**Failure scenario.** Researcher attaches a lab note to a staged dataset, later deletes the dataset. The note now shows a target to a nonexistent dataset, cannot have its targets edited (every PATCH with targets 404s), and the project graph/export contains an edge to a missing node.

**Suggested fix.** Either block deletion while notes target the entity (mirroring the question guard, one query_notes(target_entity_type=..., target_entity_id=..., limit=1) call), or prune matching note_targets rows inside the same transaction (like remove_goal_links_to_entity). For sessions also clear/guard source_session_id on staged datasets.

**Verification.** verify=confirmed(medium)

#### M56. Dataset delete guard is unlocked against concurrent claim/analysis creation on Postgres

`src/lab_tracker/services/dataset_service.py:493` · concurrency · area: data-integrity

`_ensure_dataset_can_be_deleted` reads claims and analyses referencing the dataset, but `create_claim`/`create_analysis` take no dataset lock (they only do `get_dataset`), and the deletion path only holds the dataset-file advisory locks that file writes and graph commits use. On Postgres, a claim insert that commits between the guard read and the DELETE (or that is waiting on the dataset row's KEY SHARE lock) either has its `claim_datasets` row cascaded away, silently un-supporting a SUPPORTED claim, or fails with an FK IntegrityError (500) instead of a 409. SQLite is only protected by chance ordering because the guard reads run outside the write fence.

**Evidence.** dataset_service.py:479-505 (guard) vs claim_service.py:80-145 and analysis_service.py:80-144 (no `lock_dataset_*` calls); repository.py:349-401 lock plan covers file writes only. Not reproduced (no Postgres available in this environment).

**Failure scenario.** An MCP agent records an evidence bundle creating a supported claim on staged dataset D while a user deletes D; the claim commits then D's cascade removes claim_datasets -> supported claim with zero evidence, which publication-readiness later flags as unsupported.

**Suggested fix.** Have claim/analysis creation take `lock_dataset_file_mutation` (shared) on each supporting dataset, or re-check `_ensure_dataset_can_be_deleted` after `lock_dataset_updates` and add ondelete=RESTRICT on claim_datasets/analysis_datasets so the DB rejects the loser with a mappable ConflictError.

**Verification.** verify=confirmed(medium)

#### M57. delete_exploration_node has no referrer guard or lock: deleting a node strips a committed PIVOT's exactly-one invalidation target and its parent edges

`src/lab_tracker/services/exploration_service.py:352` · data-integrity · area: services-core-b

delete_exploration_node performs no referrer check and takes no lock. When a committed PIVOT node invalidates node D and lists D as a parent, deleting D leaves the PIVOT with status=COMMITTED, invalidates_node_id=None, invalidates_claim_id=None and parent_node_ids=[] (FK SET NULL plus edge CASCADE), violating the exactly-one-invalidation-target rule that _ensure_invalidated_refs enforces on every write. question_service.delete_question likewise never consults exploration_nodes.target_entity_* (no FK), so a node's target dangles after the question is deleted. No test exercises delete_exploration_node.

**Evidence.** Reproduced by the completeness critic with a scratch test (FK pragma on, as in the project conftest): commit PIVOT invalidating D with D as parent; DELETE D; PIVOT row reads status=COMMITTED, invalidates_node_id=None, invalidates_claim_id=None, parent_node_ids=[].

**Failure scenario.** A researcher deletes an exploration node that a committed pivot depends on; the pivot silently becomes a committed record with no invalidation target, which the write-side validator would have rejected.

**Suggested fix.** Add a referrer guard (or a generic 'who references X' query, see the deletion family) to delete_exploration_node and delete_question, take the project lock, and add tests for node deletion.

**Verification.** critic=confirmed(medium)

#### M58. One dangling goal link makes goal listings fail for every caller

`src/lab_tracker/services/goal_service.py:214` · error-handling · area: services-core-a

can_read_goal wraps _require_goal_read and swallows only AuthError. _require_goal_read resolves every link target via _ensure_target_exists, which raises NotFoundError when a target row no longer exists. list_visible_goals (GET /goals, GET /projects/{id}/goals) and the route-level filtering in list_node_goals call can_read_goal for each goal, so a single goal with a dangling link turns the entire listing into a 404 'Question does not exist.' for all users. The repository's own test (test_goal_search_hides_a_dangling_link_target_as_a_missing_goal) treats dangling links as an expected state and handles it for the single-goal GET, but the list paths were not made resilient. Dangling links are reachable in production because link creation and entity deletion take no common lock, and because DB-level cascades (e.g. project deletion cascading a question) do not touch goal_links.entity_id (no FK).

**Evidence.** goal_service.py:214-219: def can_read_goal(...): try: self._require_goal_read(goal, actor=actor) except AuthError: return False return True Scratch test: create goal linked to question, delete the question row directly (same technique as tests/test_goals.py:1031-1036), then api.goals.list_visible_goals(project_id=..., actor=admin) -> lab_tracker.errors.NotFoundError: Question does not exist. (raised from goal_service.py:212 -> 216 -> 503 -> 487 -> 480 -> 468)

**Failure scenario.** A goal link to question X is created while X is being deleted (no shared lock), or a race leaves a link to a cascaded entity. Afterwards GET /goals and GET /projects/{id}/goals return 404 for every member until someone manually fixes the row.

**Suggested fix.** In can_read_goal also catch NotFoundError and return False (or skip the unresolved link in _goal_scope_project_ids for listing purposes); add a test that lists goals when one goal has a dangling link.

**Verification.** verify=confirmed(medium)

#### M59. Commit applies member-onboarding-only target rules to every link_note_to_question draft, so $ref or mixed targets fail at commit

`src/lab_tracker/services/graph_draft_commit.py:283` · correctness · area: graph-drafting

`_lock_question_update_projects` runs for every accepted operation set (not only `GraphDraftPurpose.MEMBER_CHECKPOINT_ALIGNMENT`). For any `update note` op with `semantic_type == link_note_to_question` it (a) requires every target to be a question, (b) parses each `entity_id` with `UUID(str(...))`, and (c) requires the question to be active/staged. Ordinary drafts routinely violate these: the provider instructions tell the model to reference entities created earlier in the same draft with `{"$ref": "name"}` (so a note linked to a freshly drafted question has `entity_id = {"$ref": "q1"}`), and `NoteUpdate.targets` replaces the whole target list so a model that preserves an existing session/dataset target while adding a question produces mixed targets. The validator and the accept path both approve these payloads (`_iter_payload_entity_refs` skips `$ref` dicts and `_payload_for_review_validation` substitutes a placeholder), so the reviewer accepts them, and only the owner's commit fails, with a misleading 'Onboarding ...' error. The transaction rolls back, so nothing is corrupted, but …

**Evidence.** graph_draft_commit.py L280-301: `if operation.entity_type == EntityType.NOTE and operation.op == UPDATE and operation.semantic_type == LINK_NOTE_TO_QUESTION: raw_targets = operation.payload.get('targets') ... if target.get('entity_type') != 'question': raise ValidationError('Onboarding note links require question targets.') ... question = self.questions.get_question(UUID(str(target.get('entity_id')))) except (TypeError, ValueError): raise ValidationError('Onboarding question target is invalid.')` with no purpose check. Scratch test (scratchpad/p2/test_link_ref_commit.py) against a GENERAL draft: op1 create question client_ref q1, op2 update note link_note_to_question targets [{question, …

**Failure scenario.** Model drafts 'create question q1' + 'link existing note to {$ref: q1}' exactly as instructed by `_instructions()`; reviewer accepts both; owner clicks Commit -> 422 'Onboarding question target is invalid.' and the draft stays READY/SUBMITTED. Same for a note whose targets keep an existing session alongside the new question.

**Suggested fix.** Guard the LINK_NOTE_TO_QUESTION target checks (and the second re-read loop) with `change_set.purpose == GraphDraftPurpose.MEMBER_CHECKPOINT_ALIGNMENT` (pass `change_set` or `purpose` into `_lock_question_update_projects`); for general drafts resolve `$ref`s via `ref_map`/skip them and lock only concrete question projects; add a regression test that commits a general draft linking a note to a question created in the same draft.

**Verification.** verify=confirmed(high), refute=confirmed(medium), lead=plausible(high)

#### M60. Frontend offers 'Revise' on Daily Review (batch) drafts but the backend rejects revision of graph_batch drafts

`src/lab_tracker/services/graph_draft_generation.py:696` · contract-drift · area: graph-drafting

`GraphDraftReviewCoordinator.revise_graph_change_set` delegates to `propose_note_revision`, which only handles GRAPH_CONTEXT and IMAGE_ONLY and raises `ValidationError('Unsupported graph draft mode.')` for GRAPH_BATCH. The batch detail route (`/app/batches/{id}`) renders `GraphDraftDetailCard`, whose hook computes `canReviseDraft = canEditDraft && !isMemberOnboarding` with no draft_mode check, so the AudioReviewConsole revise action is shown for every batch draft and always fails with a 422. No test exercises revise on a batch draft.

**Evidence.** graph_draft_generation.py L682-696: `if mode == GRAPH_CONTEXT ... elif mode == IMAGE_ONLY ... else: raise ValidationError('Unsupported graph draft mode.')`; frontend_src/hooks/useGraphDraftWorkflow.js L136 `const canReviseDraft = canEditDraft && !isMemberOnboarding;`; app-shell.jsx L600-611 renders GraphDraftDetailCard for route.kind === 'batch'. Scratch test (scratchpad/p2/test_batch_revise.py): run-now -> READY batch -> POST /graph-drafts/{cs}/revise feedback -> `422 {"message":"Unsupported graph draft mode."}`.

**Failure scenario.** Assignee opens a Daily Review batch, dictates 'split this into two questions' and presses Revise -> request fails with 'Unsupported graph draft mode.'; the UI offers an action the API cannot perform.

**Suggested fix.** Either implement batch revision (rebuild the batch context from `source_note_ids` and call `draft_from_batch` with the revise hint) or hide/disable revise when `changeSet.draft_mode === 'graph_batch'` and make the backend error explicit ('Batch drafts cannot be revised; reject or edit operations instead'). Add an API test for revise on a batch draft.

**Verification.** verify=confirmed(medium)

#### M61. Server-side storage read failures are reported as 422 validation errors with no traceback logged

`src/lab_tracker/services/note_service.py:661` · error-handling · area: config-failure-modes

`_request_voice_transcript` wraps any non-NotFound/non-ValidationError exception from `raw_storage.read` (OSError, PermissionError, disk/volume errors) as `ValidationError('Source audio file could not be read.')`; `graph_draft_context.py:220` does the same for image notes. `ValidationError` maps to HTTP 422 (routes/errors.py:357-369) and `_log_handled_error` (routes/errors.py:489-503) logs only `str(exc)` at WARNING with no `exc_info`, so the underlying infrastructure exception (chained via `from exc`) never reaches the logs. A server misconfiguration is thus presented to the client as their own bad request and is invisible to the operator.

**Evidence.** note_service.py:652-661 and graph_draft_context.py:213-220 `except Exception as exc: raise ValidationError(...) from exc`; routes/errors.py:496-503 warning without exc_info; ValidationError handler returns 422.

**Failure scenario.** The note-storage volume is mounted read-only or its permissions change after an upgrade; every transcription/graph-draft request returns 422 'Source audio file could not be read.' and the JSON logs contain no stack trace or errno, sending the lab down a client-side debugging path.

**Suggested fix.** Let OSError propagate (or raise a dedicated `StorageUnavailableError` mapped to 500/503) and log it with `exc_info=True`; reserve ValidationError for client-caused input problems.

**Verification.** verify=confirmed(medium)

#### M62. Note can be archived without a reason (POST/PATCH status=archived) and un-archived leaving stale archived_reason/archived_at, breaking the documented 'archiving always names a reason' invariant

`src/lab_tracker/services/note_service.py:809` · data-integrity · area: domain-models · also reported as services-core-a-2

docs/curation-states.md and docs/retained-v1-surface.md promise that 'a captured note is never silently dropped' and that setting one aside 'is a first-class action that always names a reason' via POST /notes/{id}/archive, recording archived_reason/archived_at/archived_by. But NoteCreate.status and NoteUpdate.status (schemas.py:534, 550) accept NoteStatus.ARCHIVED, and note_service.update_note simply assigns `note.status = status` with no transition table (there is no _NOTE_STATUS_TRANSITIONS in services/shared.py, unlike questions/datasets/sessions/analyses/claims) and no reason/actor stamping. The reverse is also unguarded: PATCH {status: staged} on a properly archived note resets status while archived_reason and archived_at remain set. The result is archived notes with archived_reason=null (indistinguishable from the 'reviewed vs never reviewed' distinction the docs say you can 'always tell') and staged notes that still carry archive stamps. The same PATCH surface is exposed to AI agents through the MCP write tools (mcp_tools/write.py passes `status` through to the note client).

**Evidence.** Scratch test (TestClient, admin): POST /notes {raw_content:'x', status:'archived'} -> 201, data.archived_reason == None. PATCH /notes/{id} {status:'archived'} -> 200, status 'archived', archived_reason None. Then POST /notes/{id}/archive {reason:'superseded'} -> 200 archived_reason 'superseded'; PATCH /notes/{id} {status:'staged'} -> 200 with status 'staged', archived_reason 'superseded', archived_at '2026-09-20T19:56:25Z' still set. Code: schemas.py:550 `status: NoteStatus | SkipJsonSchema[None] = None`; note_service.py:806-809 `if is_provided(status): ... note.status = status`; archive_note (note_service.py:829-858) is the only path that sets archived_reason/archived_at/archived_by.

**Failure scenario.** A contributor (or an MCP agent calling the note update tool) sends PATCH /notes/{id} {"status":"archived"}: the note disappears from the staged/committed views with archived_reason null, so the 'unreviewed since June 1' coverage accounting the docs promise cannot distinguish it from a reviewed-not-relevant capture. Conversely PATCH {"status":"staged"} on an archived note resurrects it while still reporting …

**Suggested fix.** Reject NoteStatus.ARCHIVED in NoteCreate/NoteUpdate (or make PATCH status archived route through archive_note semantics requiring a reason), add a _NOTE_STATUS_TRANSITIONS table in services/shared.py mirroring the other entities, and clear archived_* fields (or forbid the transition) when a note leaves ARCHIVED.

**Verification.** verify=confirmed(medium), refute=confirmed(medium), lead=confirmed(medium)

#### M63. Note deletion ignores committed dataset manifests and note->note targets, leaving dangling provenance references

`src/lab_tracker/services/note_service.py:903` · data-integrity · area: data-integrity

`_ensure_note_can_be_deleted` only refuses when a graph change set references the note. Committed dataset manifests are content-addressed and immutable (`Committed datasets are immutable`) yet store `note_ids` as a plain JSON list, and `note_targets.entity_id` has no FK; neither is checked. Deleting the note leaves the immutable manifest pointing at a missing note and leaves orphan `note_targets` rows for every note that targeted it.

**Evidence.** note_service.py:872-915; db_models.py DatasetModel.manifest_note_ids JSON (~421), NoteTargetModel.entity_id GUID without FK (~536). Scratch reproduction (test_note_delete_ignores_committed_manifest_and_note_targets): committed dataset with note_ids=[N] and a second note targeting N; `delete_note(N)` succeeds, manifest still lists N, one dangling note_targets row remains.

**Failure scenario.** A lab member deletes a protocol note that a committed dataset cites in its manifest; PROV export and publication-readiness now reference a note that returns 404, and the dataset's commit hash still covers the deleted evidence.

**Suggested fix.** Extend the guard to datasets whose `commit_manifest.note_ids` contain the note (at least for COMMITTED/ARCHIVED datasets) and to notes targeting it (`query_notes(target_entity_type='note', target_entity_id=note_id)`), or delete/retarget those rows explicitly.

**Verification.** verify=confirmed(medium)

#### M64. Project owner can detach a project from its group without any group authorization, severing PI oversight

`src/lab_tracker/services/project_service.py:238` · authz · area: sec-authz · also reported as member-onboarding-2

update_project requires project owner, and when a new group_id is supplied it correctly requires require_group_owner on the target group. But when group_id is provided as null the project is silently un-grouped with no check at all. The retained surface (docs/retained-v1-surface.md) makes group-owner inheritance the PI-oversight mechanism: group owners inherit owner access to every child project. A direct project owner (any student/editor who was made owner) can PATCH {group_id: null} and the group owner (PI) immediately loses read/owner access, batch oversight, group-scoped record export, and group data-store inheritance for that project. Attaching needs group-owner consent; detaching needs none, which is the asymmetric half that matters for oversight.

**Evidence.** project_service.py:230-243: `self.authorization.require_owner(project_id, actor=actor)` ... `if is_provided(group_id): if group_id is not None: require_group_owner(...)` `project.group_id = group_id` — no branch for the None case. ProjectUpdate schema (schemas.py:338) accepts `group_id: UUID | None`. Scratch test test_project_owner_can_detach_project_from_group_without_group_rights: PI creates group (owner), admin creates project in that group and adds student as project owner; PI GET /projects/{id} -> 200; student PATCH {group_id: null} -> `detach -> 200 ... "group_id":null`; PI GET afterwards -> `PI read after detach -> 404`.

**Failure scenario.** A trainee who is the direct owner of a thesis project inside the lab group removes the project from the group; the PI (group owner, no direct membership) can no longer read it, is dropped from owner-only Daily Review commit views, and the lab's group record-export/offboarding scope no longer covers that project.

**Suggested fix.** When group_id changes from a non-null value (to null or to another group), require group-owner (or global admin) authority on the *current* group as well as the target group, e.g. `if project.group_id is not None and group_id != project.group_id: self.authorization.require_group_owner(project.group_id, actor=actor)`. Add a policy test for detach.

**Verification.** verify=confirmed(medium)

#### M65. Last group owner can be demoted via PATCH/POST members, leaving a group with no owner although DELETE guards this invariant

`src/lab_tracker/services/project_service.py:453` · data-integrity · area: member-onboarding

delete_group_membership enforces 'Groups must keep at least one owner.' but upsert_group_membership (used by both POST /groups/{id}/members and PATCH /groups/{id}/members/{user_id}) changes an existing owner's role with no owner-count check. The project-membership counterpart (_update_existing_project_membership_role) does guard demotion. After the sole group owner demotes themselves, nobody except a global admin can manage the group, its members, its project memberships, or its group_read_all flag; group-owner inherited access on child projects disappears.

**Evidence.** project_service.py:453-482 has no owner-count logic; 484-500 (delete) does. Reproduced: editor creates group (auto owner), PATCH /groups/{g}/members/{self} {"role":"viewer"} -> `PATCH SELF DEMOTE 200`, `OWNERS LEFT ['viewer']`, subsequent PATCH /groups/{g} by the same user -> `PI MANAGE AFTER 401`; POST /groups/{g}/members with own username role contributor -> `POST SELF DEMOTE 201`. tests/test_group_routes.py::test_group_routes_reject_last_owner_removal_and_allow_group_delete covers DELETE only.

**Failure scenario.** PI clicks 'change role' on their own row in the lab group and picks contributor by mistake; the lab group is now ownerless, the PI loses owner access to every child project they did not own directly, and an admin must repair it.

**Suggested fix.** In upsert_group_membership, when existing.role == OWNER and role != OWNER, count owners (ideally under a group-owner row lock analogous to lock_project_owner_memberships) and raise ValidationError('Groups must keep at least one owner.') when it would drop to zero. Reuse the same helper from delete_group_membership so the invariant lives in one place.

**Verification.** verify=confirmed(medium)

#### M66. Offboarding export gate is satisfied by any prior export event, so records created after the export are never gated

`src/lab_tracker/services/project_service.py:786` · data-integrity · area: member-onboarding

_ensure_offboarding_records_released passes as soon as any RecordExportEvent for the user has a project_ids set that covers the projects with attributed records. The check ignores when the export happened versus when the records were created/updated, so one export taken early in a member's tenure permanently satisfies the 'Export or reassign this user's records before revoking membership' guard for that project. All subsequently created questions, notes, datasets, analyses, claims and visualizations are unexported yet the membership revoke succeeds silently.

**Evidence.** project_service.py:769-792: `export_events, _ = self.repository.query_record_export_events(user_id=user_id, ...)` then `for event in export_events: if record_project_ids.issubset(set(event.project_ids)): return`. RecordExportEventModel (db_models.py:1708) stores project_ids/record_counts/created_at but the guard never compares created_at to record timestamps. Reproduced with scratch test: member creates Q1 -> admin exports (export lists only Q1) -> member creates Q2 and a note -> DELETE /projects/{p}/members/{user} returned `REMOVE STATUS 200`. Existing tests (tests/test_offboarding_guard.py) only cover export-then-remove with no intervening writes.

**Failure scenario.** PI exports a student's records in March for a grant report. Student keeps working until August, then leaves. Admin revokes membership; the guard passes on the March export event, so five months of records are never captured in a sidecar export even though the UI/docs promise export-before-revoke.

**Suggested fix.** Compare freshness, not just scope: only accept an export event whose created_at is >= the max(created_at, updated_at) of every attributed record in record_project_ids (or store exported record ids/hashes in the event and require a superset). Alternatively record a per-(user, project) 'last export' watermark and require records_attributed_to_user(since=watermark) to be empty.

**Verification.** verify=confirmed(medium)

#### M67. delete_question guard misses question_refactors CASCADE and superseded links: deleting a refactor replacement erases the refactor record and strands the source question in terminal SUPERSEDED

`src/lab_tracker/services/question_service.py:764` · data-integrity · area: prior-high · also reported as services-core-a-3

_ensure_question_not_referenced checks notes, experiments, datasets (primary + links), sessions and claim answers, but not the refactor relationship. question_refactors.source_question_id and replacement_question_id are ondelete=CASCADE (db_models.py:263-271) and questions.superseded_by_question_id / supersedes_question_id are ondelete=SET NULL (db_models.py:206-213). With FK enforcement on (production SQLite via db.py pragma, and Postgres), DELETE /questions/{id} on the replacement question of a refactor succeeds, the question_refactors row (reason, source_snapshot, replacement_snapshot) is cascade-deleted, and the source question's superseded_by_question_id is nulled while its status stays SUPERSEDED, which is terminal (_QUESTION_STATUS_TRANSITIONS in services/shared.py:240). The result is an unrecoverable, successor-less superseded question and the loss of the audit record the refactor feature exists to keep (list_question_refactors route at routes/questions.py:207 and QuestionDetailCard.jsx consume it). Deleting the superseded source question has the symmetric effect (refactor …

**Evidence.** Reproduced with a scratch test using tests/api_helpers.repository_backed_api plus a global Engine connect listener setting PRAGMA foreign_keys=ON (matching src/lab_tracker/db.py and tests/conftest.py): create ACTIVE question, api.refactor_question(...) -> 1 refactor listed; api.delete_question(replacement) succeeds; afterwards list_question_refactors(source) returns 0 and get_question(source) reports status=SUPERSEDED, superseded_by_question_id=None. With FKs off the same sequence leaves a dangling superseded_by pointer instead.

**Failure scenario.** A contributor refactors question A into B (reason and snapshots recorded), later decides B was wrong and deletes B (B is unreferenced, so nothing blocks it). A is now permanently SUPERSEDED with no successor and cannot transition back to ACTIVE; the refactor reason/snapshots are gone from the audit endpoint and the question history UI with no error or warning.

**Suggested fix.** In _ensure_question_not_referenced, reject deletion when the question is the source or replacement of any question_refactors row (repository.list_question_refactors / query by either column) or when superseded_by_question_id / supersedes_question_id is set on it or on any other question pointing at it; alternatively change those FKs to RESTRICT via an Alembic migration so the DB refuses the delete. Add a test covering deletion of both the source …

**Verification.** verify=confirmed(medium), lead=confirmed(medium)

#### M68. Session deletion leaves staged datasets with a dangling manifest source_session_id and cascades captured acquisition collections

`src/lab_tracker/services/session_service.py:262` · data-integrity · area: data-integrity

`_ensure_session_can_be_deleted` only blocks when a *non-staged* dataset's manifest references the session. `datasets.manifest_source_session_id` has no FK (db_models.py:423), so after the delete a staged dataset keeps the dead id, and its later commit path (`update_dataset` -> `validate_source_session` -> `sessions.get_session`) raises `NotFoundError('Session does not exist.')`, i.e. the dataset can no longer be committed through the normal PATCH without the client rewriting the manifest. The guard also ignores `acquisition_collections` (ondelete=CASCADE via collection_db_models.py:81): every immutable snapshot/manifest captured for the session is deleted with it by a contributor-level action.

**Evidence.** session_service.py:243-267 and db_models.py:423. Scratch reproduction (test_session_delete_leaves_staged_dataset_with_dangling_source_session): promote operational session to a STAGED dataset, delete session (allowed), reload dataset -> `source_session_id` still set; `update_dataset(status=COMMITTED)` raises NotFoundError 'Session does not exist.'

**Failure scenario.** Acquisition script promotes a session to a staged dataset, someone tidies up the session, later the dataset commit returns 404 'Session does not exist' on a PATCH /datasets/{id}; the promoted dataset's provenance points at nothing and any collection snapshots for the session are gone.

**Suggested fix.** Treat any dataset whose manifest references the session (regardless of status) and any acquisition collection under it as blocking references (or clear/retarget explicitly); consider a real FK on manifest_source_session_id with ondelete RESTRICT/SET NULL so the DB enforces it.

**Verification.** verify=confirmed(medium)

#### M69. Questions can be marked superseded with no replacement pointer via PATCH/POST, and can be created directly in answered/abandoned/superseded, bypassing the transition table

`src/lab_tracker/services/shared.py:225` · data-integrity · area: domain-models

_QUESTION_STATUS_TRANSITIONS admits STAGED->SUPERSEDED and ACTIVE->SUPERSEDED for a plain status PATCH, but only the refactor flow (question_service.py:595-646) sets superseded_by_question_id / supersedes_question_id and records a QuestionRefactor. A plain PATCH {status:'superseded'} therefore produces a terminal 'superseded' question with superseded_by_question_id=null and no refactor record, which is exactly the state the refactor code guards against ('Question has already been superseded.' at line 606 checks the pointer, not the status). Separately, QuestionCreate.status (schemas.py:425) accepts any QuestionStatus at creation, so POST /questions can create a question already 'answered' or 'superseded' even though the transition table forbids STAGED->ANSWERED (must pass through ACTIVE). terminal_reason enforcement only covers ABANDONED (question_service.py:177-182), so superseded/answered need no justification either.

**Evidence.** Scratch test: PATCH /questions/{id} {status:'superseded'} -> 200 with status 'superseded' and superseded_by_question_id None. POST /questions {..., status:'superseded'} -> 201 status 'superseded'. POST /questions {..., status:'answered'} -> 201 (whereas PATCH staged->answered is rejected by the transition table). Code: shared.py:225-240 includes QuestionStatus.SUPERSEDED in the STAGED and ACTIVE transition sets; question_service.py:481-497 only calls _ensure_question_status_transition and terminal_reason_for_patch(..., QuestionStatus.ABANDONED, ...).

**Failure scenario.** A user or agent PATCHes a question to 'superseded' by hand. The graph now shows a superseded question that points nowhere; QuestionDetail 'superseded by' lookups, the refactor guard, publication-readiness ungrounded-question checks and the PROV export all treat superseded as 'replaced by a later question' and either render a dangling state or later allow a second refactor on it because the pointer is still null.

**Suggested fix.** Remove SUPERSEDED from the plain-PATCH transition sets (only the refactor command may set it), and restrict QuestionCreate.status to STAGED/ACTIVE (or run the same transition table from an implicit STAGED start), requiring terminal_reason for any terminal creation status.

**Verification.** verify=confirmed(medium)

#### M70. Dataset commit manifests accept nonexistent and cross-project note_ids

`src/lab_tracker/services/shared.py:658` · data-integrity · area: services-core-a

build_commit_manifest only de-duplicates note_ids (unique_ids); neither create_dataset nor _update_dataset_in_transaction verifies that each note exists or belongs to the dataset's project, even though the note_ids become part of the content-addressed, immutable committed manifest and of the PROV-O export. Every other cross-entity reference in this layer (question links, source session, note targets, goal links) is validated for existence and project scope.

**Evidence.** shared.py:658: note_ids=unique_ids(manifest_input.note_ids) dataset_service.py:149-153 and 401-405 call build_commit_manifest with no note validation. Scratch test: create_dataset(status=COMMITTED, commit_manifest=DatasetCommitManifestInput(files=[...], note_ids=[uuid4(), <note from another project>])) succeeded: NOTE_IDS ACCEPTED: [UUID('04ae8e8e-...'), UUID('bde8ec19-...')]

**Failure scenario.** An agent commits a dataset in project A whose manifest lists a note id from project B (or a random UUID). The committed hash permanently embeds a cross-project/dangling reference; graph views and exports that dereference manifest note_ids either leak the foreign id or hit missing rows.

**Suggested fix.** In create_dataset/_update_dataset_in_transaction, resolve each manifest note id via notes.get_note and require note.project_id == dataset.project_id (ValidationError otherwise), mirroring validate_source_session.

**Verification.** verify=confirmed(medium)

#### M71. Any global editor can create, edit, delete and list supervision edges between arbitrary users

`src/lab_tracker/services/supervision_service.py:29` · authz · area: sec-authz · also reported as member-onboarding-4

Supervision edges (who supervises whom, with start/end dates) are guarded only by require_role(actor, WRITE_ROLES) for every operation, with no relation to the actor (neither endpoint must be the actor, no group ownership). Any editor on the instance can assert or delete a supervision relationship between any two users and list every edge, and those edges are embedded into record-export provenance documents (record_export_service.py:96). In a shared multi-user deployment this lets a non-admin rewrite the lab's provenance/oversight structure and enumerate all supervisor/supervisee pairs.

**Evidence.** supervision_service.py:29,54,72,92,126 — each of create/get/list/update/delete calls only `require_role(actor, WRITE_ROLES)`; routes/supervision.py passes through unchanged; record_export_service.py:96 `supervision_edges, _ = repository.query_supervision_edges(limit=None, offset=0)` feeds the PROV-O export.

**Failure scenario.** An editor creates an edge naming themselves as supervisor of the PI (or deletes the real PI→student edge); subsequent user record exports and provenance documents carry the falsified supervision lineage.

**Suggested fix.** Restrict create/update/delete to global admins or to group owners of a group containing both users, and restrict list/get for non-admins to edges involving the actor (or users in groups they own).

**Verification.** impact-single=refuted(low), verify[member-onboarding-4]=confirmed(medium), lead-ruling=confirmed(medium)

#### M72. Visualization metadata PATCH rewrites asset_* columns from a stale snapshot without the row lock the upload path uses, so it can revert a concurrently committed upload to an already-deleted blob

`src/lab_tracker/services/visualization_service.py:180` · concurrency · area: services-core-b

`update_visualization` loads the pydantic `Visualization` (including `asset`), mutates viz_type/caption/etc., and calls `repository.visualizations.save(visualization)`. `apply_visualization_to_model` (sqlalchemy_mappers.py:1533-1548) unconditionally writes `row.asset_storage_id/filename/content_type/size_bytes/checksum` from `visualization.asset`. The upload command (`VisualizationFileCommands.upload`, file_commands.py:453) takes `locked_visualization_row` (SELECT ... FOR UPDATE) and, on replace, schedules deletion of the old storage id after commit. The PATCH path takes no lock and does no compare-and-set, so if an upload commits between the PATCH's read and its UPDATE, the PATCH's UPDATE overwrites the asset columns with the previous storage id whose blob was just deleted; the new blob becomes an orphan and `download` fails. The dedicated race tests (test_postgres_visualization_file_races.py) cover upload-vs-upload and upload-vs-delete only.

**Evidence.** visualization_service.py:142-181: `visualization = self.get_visualization(viz_id)` ... `with self.unit_of_work() as repository: repository.visualizations.save(visualization)` with no lock. sqlalchemy_mappers.py:1538-1548 `row.asset_storage_id = (_uuid_str(visualization.asset.storage_id) if visualization.asset is not None else None)` etc. file_commands.py:450-455 comment 'Acquire the row lock before any durable storage write'; :524-531 `run_after_commit(delete_stored_visualization_file(old_storage_id))`.

**Failure scenario.** Postgres, two users: U1 PATCHes the caption of a figure (reads row with asset A). U2 uploads a replacement figure (locks row, sets asset B, commits, blob A deleted after commit). U1's UPDATE then applies, restoring asset_storage_id=A. GET /visualizations/{id}/file/download now points at a deleted object; blob B is orphaned.

**Suggested fix.** In `update_visualization` (or the repository save), never write asset columns from the domain snapshot: split asset metadata out of `apply_visualization_to_model`, or lock the row (`locked_visualization_row`) and re-read before applying the patch, and add a race test analogous to test_postgres_conditional_replacement_race_rejects_stale_loser_without_orphan.

**Verification.** verify=confirmed(medium)

#### M73. created_by list filters bind an arbitrary client string to a GUID column, turning a non-UUID value into an unhandled StatementError (HTTP 500)

`src/lab_tracker/sqlalchemy_repository_parts/core.py:502` · error-handling · area: repository

All `query()` methods compare `created_by_user_id == created_by` where `created_by` is the raw `str` from the `CreatedByFilter` query parameter (routes/shared.py declares it `str | None`, not `UUID`). `created_by_user_id` is a `GUID` TypeDecorator whose `process_bind_param` calls `UUID(str(value))`, so any non-UUID string raises `ValueError` inside statement execution, which SQLAlchemy wraps as `StatementError`. Nothing in the route/application layer catches it. The same pattern exists in notes.py:560, datasets.py:133, sessions.py:70, analyses.py:165/318/326/334/592 and exploration.py:146. A username (the natural thing for an MCP agent or CLI user to pass) therefore produces a 500 instead of a 422 or an empty page.

**Evidence.** Scratch test using the project's `client`/`admin_auth_headers` fixtures: `client.get('/questions', params={'created_by': 'alice'}, headers=admin_auth_headers)` raises through the TestClient: sqlalchemy.exc.StatementError: (builtins.ValueError) badly formed hexadecimal UUID string [SQL: SELECT ... FROM questions WHERE questions.project_id IN (...) AND questions.created_by_user_id = ?] With `raise_server_exceptions=False` this is a 500 response. `grep -rn StatementError src/lab_tracker/app*` finds no handler.

**Failure scenario.** Any authenticated caller (UI, lt CLI, hosted MCP agent) requests `GET /notes?created_by=alice` or `GET /questions?created_by=me`; the server returns 500 and logs a stack trace instead of rejecting the parameter.

**Suggested fix.** Validate `created_by` as a UUID at the boundary (declare `CreatedByFilter` as `UUID | None` or parse and return 422), and/or have the repository query methods accept `UUID | None` and convert once; consider making `GUID.process_bind_param` raise a typed error that the API maps to 422.

**Verification.** verify=confirmed(medium)

#### M74. Lease-expiry reclaims are unbounded: max_attempts is enforced only when a worker reports failure

`src/lab_tracker/sqlalchemy_repository_parts/graph_batches.py:463` · data-integrity · area: review-email-scheduling

claim_next() re-leases any SENDING row whose lease_expires_at has passed and increments attempt_count, but neither the repository nor ReviewEmailService.claim_next() compares attempt_count with max_attempts; the bound is checked only in mark_failed(). A delivery whose worker repeatedly dies between claim and accepted/failed (external automation crashing, DB write failing in mark_accepted, a claim JSON that the mailbox automation never acts on) is re-leased every lease period forever with an ever-growing attempt_count. With the SMTP transport that means unbounded resends when mark_accepted fails after a successful send; with the external transport it is a permanent poison row that every scheduled `claim` run picks up again. docs/daily-review-email-alerts.md:21-23 advertises 'a bounded attempt count'.

**Evidence.** Scratch test with review_email_max_attempts=2: enqueue_test, then claim_next(lease_seconds=60, now=t+61*i) six times -> every call returns the same row: [(SENDING,1),(SENDING,2),(SENDING,3),(SENDING,4),(SENDING,5),(SENDING,6)]. graph_batches.py:463-468 `stale_claim = and_(status == SENDING, lease_expires_at.is_not(None), lease_expires_at <= now)`; :495 `attempt_count=ReviewEmailOutboxModel.attempt_count + 1`; review_email_service.py:209 `terminal = not retryable or delivery.attempt_count >= self.max_attempts` is the only bound.

**Failure scenario.** External transport: the mailbox automation crashes after `claim` (or a delivery's claim JSON is lost). Every 300 s the row is re-leased and handed out again indefinitely; the queue never drains that row and no operator-visible FAILED state is ever recorded.

**Suggested fix.** Exclude rows with attempt_count >= max_attempts from the eligible predicate (or have ReviewEmailService.claim_next fail them with a 'lease expired too many times' error when a reclaimed row already reached the bound) so poison deliveries reach FAILED and become visible in /review-email/deliveries.

**Verification.** verify=confirmed(medium)

#### M75. Re-drafting a note after its draft was rejected returns the rejected change set instead of generating a new draft

`src/lab_tracker/sqlalchemy_repository_parts/graph_drafts.py:468` · correctness · area: graph-drafting

Note-scoped drafts are keyed by `_note_generation_key` (project, note, source versions, mode, prompt version, hint, checksum). `claim_for_generation` only reclaims a keyed row when it is FAILED or an expired DRAFTING lease; every other status (including REJECTED, CHANGES_REQUESTED, COMMITTED) is returned as-is with `acquired=False`, and `create_graph_draft_from_note` returns that row with HTTP 201. After an owner rejects a draft, `revise` is also blocked (`_ensure_graph_change_set_editable` raises for REJECTED). So unless the note itself or the user hint changes, the 'Draft graph update' button silently returns the rejected draft forever; there is no path to a fresh proposal for that note.

**Evidence.** graph_drafts.py L468-477: `eligible = or_(status == FAILED, and_(status == DRAFTING, lease expired))`; graph_draft_generation.py L272-274: `claim = self.claim_generation(...); if not claim.acquired: return claim.change_set`; graph_draft_review.py L732-738 blocks REJECTED from edit/revise. Scratch test (scratchpad/p2/test_redraft_after_reject.py): draft -> submit -> review rejected -> POST /notes/{id}/graph-drafts again -> `REDRAFT -> 201 True rejected` (same change_set_id, status rejected).

**Failure scenario.** Contributor drafts an image note; owner reviews and rejects with a note asking for a different framing; contributor presses 'Draft graph update' again -> gets the same rejected change set (201). Revise is refused for rejected drafts, so the note is a dead end until its content is edited.

**Suggested fix.** Treat terminal, non-committed statuses (REJECTED, and arguably CHANGES_REQUESTED-with-explicit-redraft) as reclaimable in `claim_for_generation` (or include a per-request nonce/attempt in the generation key once a row is REJECTED), and return 200 rather than 201 when an existing row is rejoined; add a test for reject -> redraft.

**Verification.** verify=confirmed(medium)

#### M76. Ownership reassignment leaves experiments, exploration nodes, provenance links, data stores, entity versions and claim edges attributed to the departing user

`src/lab_tracker/sqlalchemy_repository_parts/ownership.py:38` · data-integrity · area: repository · also reported as services-core-b-2

`reassign()` rewrites attribution only for the models enumerated in `_CREATED_BY_TARGETS`/`_EXECUTED_BY_TARGETS`. Eight ORM models that carry `created_by`/`created_by_user_id` are missing: ExperimentModel, ExperimentSessionModel, ExperimentDatasetModel, EntityVersionModel, ClaimEdgeModel, ProvenanceLinkModel, ExplorationNodeModel and DataStoreModel. The offboarding contract (docs/vision.md: 'a departing member's attributed records must be exported or reassigned before their access is revoked') and `records_attributed_to_user()` (which does export exploration nodes and claim edges) therefore disagree with what reassignment moves: after a successful reassignment the departing user still owns experiments and exploration nodes, and a subsequent record export for that user still returns them. The existing test `test_ownership_reassignment_moves_all_attribution_surfaces` (tests/test_ownership_reassignments.py:69) hard-codes the same list instead of deriving it from the ORM metadata, so the gap is invisible to CI.

**Evidence.** Scratch test (repository_backed_api, admin actor = departing user) creates a question, an experiment and an exploration node, then calls `api.reassign_ownership(from_user_id, to_user_id)`. Output: record_counts: {'project_groups': 0, 'projects': 1, 'questions': 1, ..., 'analyses': 0} (no key for experiments/exploration_nodes) question: <to_user> <to_user> experiment: <from_user> <from_user> node: <from_user> <from_user> still attributed exploration nodes: 1 Model inventory: `awk '/^class .*Model\(Base\)/{cls=$2} /created_by_user_id: Mapped/{print cls}' db_models.py` lists 25 models; `_CREATED_BY_TARGETS` covers 15 (+ AnalysisModel via executed_by).

**Failure scenario.** Admin reassigns a departing trainee's records to a successor and revokes access. The trainee's experiments, exploration dead-ends/pivots, provenance-link decisions, registered data stores and claim edges keep `created_by_user_id = departing user`; `GET /users/{id}/records` export for the departing user still lists exploration nodes, and any later user deletion nulls the FK leaving dangling `created_by` text.

**Suggested fix.** Derive the target list from `Base.metadata` (every mapped table with both `created_by` and `created_by_user_id` columns, plus `executed_by`), or at minimum add the eight missing models; add a test that asserts the reassignment target set equals the set of attribution-bearing tables discovered from metadata.

**Verification.** verify=confirmed(medium)

#### M77. Every outbox sync (including the post-commit hook) downloads the project's entire note list to build the evidence index

`src/lab_tracker_client/client.py:1136` · performance · area: client-package

`build_evidence_note_index` paginates `/notes?project_id=...` in 200-record pages, and each of the three adapters calls it once per project per sync before uploading anything (watch.py:995, repo.py:875, hpc.py:767; `lt import-folder` too). `lt git snapshot` — run inline by the post-commit hook with no limit — therefore performs ⌈N/200⌉ sequential GETs of full note records (including `raw_content`) on every commit, and the scheduled `lt watch run` does the same every interval, even when there is exactly one pending event. Cost grows linearly with the project's note count (which every capture increases), so commit latency and server load degrade over the life of a project; a 15 s per-request timeout on a slow link turns into a multi-minute hook.

**Evidence.** client.py:1134-1140: ``` def build_evidence_note_index(self, *, project_id: str) -> EvidenceNoteIndex: index: EvidenceNoteIndex = {} for note in self._iter_all("/notes", params={"project_id": str(project_id)}): ``` cli.py:1408-1414 (hook path drains the whole outbox synchronously); watch.py:994-996 `note_indexes[project_id] = client.build_evidence_note_index(project_id=project_id)`; `find_note_by_marker`/`find_evidence_note` (client.py:1109, 1129) are also full scans. The server note list API exposes no metadata/evidence-key filter, so there is no cheaper lookup today.

**Failure scenario.** A project accumulates 6,000 staged/committed notes (commit snapshots, figures, watch captures). Each `git commit` in an enrolled repo now issues 30 sequential page requests (each returning full note bodies) before the one upload; on a LAN server that is several seconds per commit, on a hosted instance over VPN tens of seconds, and the scheduled watch job repeats it every 15 minutes for every enrolled machine.

**Suggested fix.** Add a server-side lookup for the evidence key (e.g. `GET /notes?evidence_source_external_id=...&evidence_content_hash=...` or a `metadata_filter`) and use it in `import_evidence_file`/`_sync_*`; or keep a local per-project index cache in the outbox refreshed incrementally with `since=`. Also consider bounding the hook's inline drain (`limit`) so a commit never pays for the whole backlog.

**Verification.** verify=confirmed(medium)

#### M78. `lt hpc finish --log` reads the whole log file into memory to extract a 4 KB tail

`src/lab_tracker_client/hpc.py:1091` · performance · area: client-package

`_read_log_excerpt` does `path.read_text(...)` and then slices the last `remaining` characters. Slurm stdout/stderr logs of long analyses are routinely hundreds of MB to several GB; `lt hpc finish --log slurm-<id>.out` is documented to run inside the job's epilogue, where memory is limited by the cgroup. Decoding to `str` roughly doubles the footprint. If the process is OOM-killed or exceeds the job's wall time, the finish event is never written (it is only written after the excerpt is built), so the run's completion provenance is lost — the opposite of the adapter's 'never loses the event' contract.

**Evidence.** hpc.py:1083-1097: ``` try: text = path.read_text(encoding="utf-8", errors="replace") except OSError: continue excerpt = text[-remaining:] ``` Scratch measurement with a 50 MiB log: `excerpt chars: 4079 peak MiB: 100.0` (tracemalloc peak).

**Failure scenario.** A job writes a 3 GB progress log; its script ends with `lt hpc finish --run "$LAB_TRACKER_HPC_RUN_ID" --exit-code $status --log slurm-$SLURM_JOB_ID.out` under a 2 GB memory cgroup. `lt` is OOM-killed, no finish event is queued, and the run stays 'begin' forever in Lab Tracker.

**Suggested fix.** Seek to `max(0, size - 4*max_chars)` and read only the tail bytes before decoding (`with path.open('rb') as fh: fh.seek(-n, os.SEEK_END)`), and write the finish event before/independently of any optional enrichment failure.

**Verification.** verify=confirmed(medium)

#### M79. 1-second git timeout silently records a dirty working tree as clean (`git_dirty: False`) in repo, HPC and figure provenance

`src/lab_tracker_client/repo.py:1137` · data-integrity · area: client-package

`repo._git_output`, `hpc._git_output` and `figure._git_output` all run git with `timeout=1` and turn `TimeoutExpired` (caught by `except Exception`) into `""`. `git_dirty = bool(_git_output(root, "status", "--porcelain"))` therefore becomes `False` whenever `git status` takes longer than one second, while the fast `rev-parse HEAD` still succeeds, so the event carries a real commit SHA plus a false 'clean tree' claim. `git status` routinely exceeds 1 s on large repos, cold caches, and especially on HPC parallel/network filesystems (Lustre/GPFS/NFS), which is exactly where `lt hpc` runs. The docs (`repo-report-capture.md` 'Dirty working trees are recorded as a flag') and the curation bridge (`Analysis.code_version` from the commit) rely on this flag being honest; a wrong 'clean' flag makes uncommitted analysis code look reproducible from the pinned commit.

**Evidence.** repo.py:55 `_GIT_TIMEOUT_SECONDS = 1`; repo.py:1130-1143: ``` result = subprocess.run(["git", "-C", str(root), *args], ..., timeout=_GIT_TIMEOUT_SECONDS) except Exception: return "" ``` hpc.py:1007-1017 (`timeout=1`, same `except Exception: return ""`), figure.py:953-966 (same), figure.py:268 `git_dirty=bool(_git_output("status", "--porcelain"))`. Scratch reproduction: dirty repo (`git status --porcelain` non-empty), PATH shim making `git status` sleep 2 s: ``` repo.git_context: {'git_commit': '596dbff7...', 'git_dirty': False} hpc.git_context: {'git_commit': '596dbff7...', 'git_dirty': False} ```

**Failure scenario.** On a cluster login node the analysis checkout lives on Lustre; `git status` takes 3 s. A researcher edits `run_analysis.py` without committing and runs `lt hpc submit -- sbatch job.sbatch`. The submit/finish events and the staged note say `Git commit: <sha>`, `Git dirty: False`, and later curation pins `Analysis.code_version` to a commit that does not contain the code that produced the results.

**Suggested fix.** Distinguish 'git failed/timed out' from 'clean': have `_git_output` return `None` on timeout/non-zero exit, record `git_dirty: null` plus a `git_status_error` string (and render 'unknown'), and raise the timeout to something realistic (10-30 s) or make it configurable. Consolidate the three copies of `_git_output`/`git_context` into one helper.

**Verification.** verify=confirmed(medium)

#### M80. Stale watch events are retried forever and consume the `--limit` budget, starving pending events on scheduled runs

`src/lab_tracker_client/watch.py:957` · correctness · area: client-package

`_sync_event` marks an event whose file/manifest changed since the scan as `sync.status = "stale"`, but `TERMINAL_SYNC_STATES = {"synced"}` so `_is_actionable` returns True for it on every later sync. Each run re-checks the fingerprint, re-records the failure (attempts++), and — because `drain_outbox` counts it as an actionable record — spends one unit of `limit` on it. Files are processed in sorted filename order (hashed capture ids), so once at least `limit` permanently-stale events sort before a pending event, `lt watch run --limit N` / `lt watch sync --limit N` (the documented scheduler form) never reaches it. Nothing prunes stale events; a modified file also gets a fresh event on the next scan, so stale events accumulate monotonically in normal use (any file rewritten between scan and sync).

**Evidence.** watch.py:60 `TERMINAL_SYNC_STATES = {"synced"}`; watch.py:954-965: ``` stale_reason = _stale_reason(event) if stale_reason: if not dry_run: _record_sync_failure(path, event, stale_reason, dry_run=False, status="stale") return WatchSyncResult(action="stale", ...) ``` outbox.py:246-253 counts every actionable record against `limit` before `process` runs. Scratch reproduction (two files rewritten after scan → two stale events, one fresh pending event, `sync_outbox(limit=2)` three times): ``` run 2: [('stale', ...), ('skipped', 'already_synced'), ('skipped', 'already_synced'), ('stale', ...)] {... 'file-8e058af8c35ef84e.json': 'pending', ... two files: 'stale'} ``` the pending event is never …

**Failure scenario.** A lab schedules `lt watch run --config ... --limit 20` every 15 minutes on a results folder where an acquisition program rewrites files after they were first scanned. After 20 such rewrites the outbox holds 20 permanently-stale events that sort first; every scheduled run spends its whole budget re-failing them and no new capture is ever synced, while `lt watch status` shows a growing `pending` count.

**Suggested fix.** Make `stale` terminal (add to `TERMINAL_SYNC_STATES`, report it as skipped/stale without spending the limit), and/or have `scan_watch` retire older events for the same capture_id when it writes a new one (or add an `lt outbox prune` for stale/quarantined records). Add a test asserting a pending event behind N stale events still syncs under `limit=N`.

**Verification.** verify=confirmed(medium)

#### M81. No test asserts ORM metadata matches the Alembic head; 32 drift items exist and most unit tests run on create_all schemas

`tests/api_helpers.py:206` · migration-drift · area: tests-quality · also reported as db-models-migrations-5

`repository_backed_api()` (used by ~30 test modules) and 20+ other tests including all of tests/test_auth_routes.py build their schema with `Base.metadata.create_all` rather than `alembic upgrade head`. Nothing in the suite runs `alembic.autogenerate.compare_metadata` against a migrated database, and running it shows 32 differences: the ORM declares `ForeignKey("users.user_id", ondelete="SET NULL")` on `notes.archived_by_user_id` (src/lab_tracker/db_models.py:534) but migration 0046 added the column as a bare String(36) with no FK; 22 columns are NOT NULL in the ORM but nullable in the migrated schema (e.g. claim_edges.created_at, group_memberships.created_at/updated_at, datasets.manifest_* JSON, data_stores.capabilities); and 9 indexes exist only in migrations (dataset_files.dataset_id, device_tokens.user_id, graph_draft_batch_runs status/window indexes, etc.). Consequently create_all-based tests exercise constraints production databases do not have, and production has indexes tests never exercise; CLAUDE.md states Alembic owns the schema, so the ORM is silently wrong.

**Evidence.** Scratch script `uv run --no-sync python compare_metadata.py cmp.db` (alembic upgrade head on SQLite, then `compare_metadata(MigrationContext, Base.metadata)`) printed `32 diffs` including `('add_fk', ForeignKeyConstraint(..., ondelete='SET NULL', table=Table('notes', ... Column('archived_by_user_id', ..., ForeignKey('users.user_id'))))` and `('modify_nullable', None, 'claim_edges', 'created_at', {...}, True, False)`. `grep -rn compare_metadata tests/` returns nothing; `grep -n create_all tests/*.py` lists api_helpers.py:206, test_auth_routes.py:40, test_persistence_flows.py, test_observability.py, test_dolt_mirror.py, test_graph_query_scale.py and others.

**Failure scenario.** A developer adds `nullable=False` or a FK to a db_models column and writes tests via repository_backed_api; the suite passes because create_all applies the constraint, but production databases (migrated) never get it, so NULLs or dangling user references are accepted in deployed instances. Conversely a migration-only index is dropped by mistake and no test notices.

**Suggested fix.** Add a test that upgrades a fresh SQLite (and, under the postgres marker, Postgres) database to head and asserts `compare_metadata(...) == []` (with an explicit, reviewed allow-list if any diff is intentional), then reconcile the current 32 diffs with a new migration or ORM edits. Consider making repository_backed_api build its schema from Alembic on a shared cached copy so unit tests run on the production schema.

**Verification.** verify=confirmed(medium)

#### M82. Three Postgres race tests lack the `postgres` marker and never run in CI

`tests/test_graph_draft_generation_fencing.py:363` · test-quality · area: tests-quality

`test_postgres_first_claim_race_reclaim_and_stale_completion_are_fenced` (this file) and `test_postgres_first_capture_serializes_with_manual_alignment` / `test_postgres_manual_link_serializes_against_question_delete` (tests/test_member_onboarding.py:957 and :1055) consume the `migrated_postgres_database_url` / `postgres_client` fixtures but carry no `@pytest.mark.postgres`. The default CI job has no Postgres URL, so conftest's fixture calls `pytest.skip`; the dedicated `postgres` job runs `uv run pytest -q -m postgres`, which deselects unmarked tests. There is no `pytest_collection_modifyitems` hook that auto-marks fixture users. These are the only Postgres tests for generation-lease fencing and onboarding manual-alignment serialization, so those invariants are unverified on the production database engine.

**Evidence.** `uv run --no-sync pytest tests/test_graph_draft_generation_fencing.py tests/test_member_onboarding.py -m postgres --collect-only -q -n0` -> "no tests collected (22 deselected)"; without the marker filter and without the URL: "3 skipped, 19 deselected". `grep -c "pytest.mark.postgres\|pytestmark"` returns 0 for both files while all other postgres-fixture users have the marker; .github/workflows/ci.yml postgres job runs `uv run pytest -q -m postgres`.

**Failure scenario.** A regression in the Postgres reclaim/fencing SQL (e.g. a lost `FOR UPDATE` or a wrong attempt_count predicate) or in onboarding manual-link serialization passes CI green on both jobs because the only tests that would catch it are skipped in one job and deselected in the other.

**Suggested fix.** Add `pytestmark`/`@pytest.mark.postgres` to the three tests, and add a conftest `pytest_collection_modifyitems` hook that applies the `postgres` marker to any item requesting `migrated_postgres_database_url`, `postgres_app`, `postgres_client` or `postgres_admin_auth_headers`, plus a small test asserting no such item is unmarked.

**Verification.** verify=confirmed(medium)

#### M83. uv.lock is stale relative to pyproject (mcp specifier) and CI/Docker never detect lock drift

`uv.lock:1006` · packaging · area: deploy-packaging · also reported as lead-1

The lock's `[package.metadata] requires-dist` still records `mcp >=1.27,<2` while pyproject.toml says `<3`. Every consumer (`.github/workflows/ci.yml`, Dockerfile, release.sh via Dockerfile) runs `uv sync --frozen`, which never validates the lock against pyproject, so drift is invisible. Any plain `uv run`/`uv sync` (as the README and docs recommend) silently rewrites uv.lock; there is no `uv lock --check` gate.

**Evidence.** `uv lock --check` in a clean `git archive HEAD` copy: `The lockfile at uv.lock needs to be updated, but --locked was provided.` Re-locking changes exactly one line: `- { name = "mcp", specifier = ">=1.27,<2" }` -> `+ { name = "mcp", specifier = ">=1.27,<3" }`. ci.yml uses `uv sync --frozen` in every job and has no `uv lock --check` step.

**Failure scenario.** A dependabot PR edits pyproject without regenerating uv.lock (this happened in #195); CI passes because --frozen ignores the mismatch; developers who run `uv run ...` get an unexpected modified uv.lock in their worktree, and release.sh (which refuses dirty worktrees) blocks until they figure out why.

**Suggested fix.** Regenerate uv.lock and add `uv lock --check` (or `uv sync --locked`) as a CI step in the `test` job.

**Verification.** verify=confirmed(medium), lead=confirmed(medium)

## Area assessments


### Application layer (`application-layer`)

The application layer is a genuinely well-separated seam: middleware opens one SQLAlchemy session per request, `LabTrackerRequestScope` owns commit/rollback and the deferred-action queues, and `RequestHandlers.compose` binds typed owners (CatalogQueries, ContextQueries, StoreHealthQueries, DatasetFileCommands, VisualizationFileCommands, ManagedDeletionCommands) to exactly that session and facade. Transaction boundaries are sound in HTTP mode: services never commit inside a request (RepositoryUnitOfWork checks `owns_active_boundary()`), direct/background APIs get a single `ApplicationTransaction`, savepoints isolate recoverable uniqueness races, and the atomicity test suite exercises both modes including commit failure and hook reuse. File commands order storage and DB writes carefully (row lock/advisory lock -> blob write -> row flush -> after_rollback cleanup; deletes flush first and unlink after commit), and managed deletions take a stable lock order (project exclusive advisory -> analyses FOR UPDATE -> visualizations FOR UPDATE) and pre-collect storage ids before the FK cascade removes the rows. Authorization composition in catalog and context queries is correct: `None` vs empty-set semantics for accessible projects are preserved end to end, targeted reads use the opaque `*_for_read` methods, and cross-project references are validated at service level so unauthorized `get_*` lookups inside provenance assembly cannot widen visibility. The api_parts mixins are thin delegates; the graph-drafts mixin is fully typed while most others are `*args/**kwargs` shims, which the typing guard tolerates only for the handler-facing subset. The fragile spots are at the edges of the deferred-action mechanism: the after-commit usage-event write for project deletion violates its own FK (reproduced), the facade's `run_after_commit/run_after_rollback` differ from the service versions outside a request, and failed blob deletions are logged but never reconciled. Documentation in docs/internal-boundaries.md is largely accurate, but the 'telemetry fires exactly once per external call' and 'routes never obtain a session' claims are not fully enforced by code or guards.

Strengths:
- Single, explicit request transaction: LabTrackerRequestScope + RepositoryUnitOfWork guarantee services never commit mid-request, and the direct-mode ApplicationTransaction nests/commits exactly once (verified by tests/test_application_transaction_atomicity.py).
- File command atomicity is well designed: locks before blob writes, blob cleanup on flush failure, after_rollback cleanup for new blobs and after_commit deletion of old/removed blobs; existing route tests cover commit-failure cleanup for dataset files.
- Managed deletions pre-collect dataset-file, note-raw and visualization storage ids under a stable lock order before the FK cascade and defer unlinking until after commit; tests cover project/analysis/visualization deletes removing managed files.
- Authorization scoping in CatalogQueries/ContextQueries preserves the empty-set-means-no-access invariant, delegates to opaque *_for_read reads, and the repository queries honor empty id sets (question_ids/note_ids/project_ids) as 'nothing'.
- External-artifact resolution and store-health flows release the DB scope before host I/O, use single-use private authorization records, and have extensive fail-closed tests (46 tests across the two context_queries suites).
- Architecture guards (test_route_application_boundary, test_static_type_boundaries, test_request_handler_api_typing, tests/typing/contracts.py under mypy strict) mechanically enforce most of the documented seam and pass on the current tree.

### External artifact resolution (`artifact-resolution`)

The external-artifact resolution area is one of the most carefully engineered parts of the codebase. The request/response boundary is layered exactly as the design document describes: Pydantic-validated bounds at the route, a second ArtifactContentBounds check in the application, opaque entity-read authorization before any artifact selection, store lookup only after that, and a detached, factory-token-sealed target that crosses the database-scope release before any network or subprocess work. Every remote target constructor requires a StoreAuthorityUseProof produced by revalidating the persisted grant against the fixed startup registry snapshot, and I found no application or MCP path that reaches a resolver adapter without one (direct resolver methods are genuinely library-only; the MCP tool goes through the HTTP API). The outbound HTTP policy is sound against the usual SSRF vectors: scheme allowlist, userinfo/backslash/control-character rejection, IDNA-normalised hostnames, single-label/.local/.localhost/.home.arpa denial, IP-literal and DNS-answer classification that forbids link-local/metadata, unspecified, multicast, reserved, IPv4-mapped/6to4/Teredo/NAT64 addresses, mixed-scope answer sets denied, conjunctive authority+CIDR internal exceptions, connections pinned to the vetted sockaddr with logical hostname retained for Host/SNI/cert checks, no proxy env, and per-hop reauthorisation of redirects with a finite hop limit. Byte and time budgets are enforced end to end: one monotonic OutboundHttpDeadline covers DNS, connect, TLS, headers, every redirect and the body via the reclamping socket facade, the 64 MiB fetch cap is applied to declared Content-Length and to actual streamed bytes, only the selected view (<=8 MiB) is retained in memory, and the sanitize postcondition re-derives truncation/size and re-hashes complete views. Admission middleware sits correctly between auth and the DB session middleware and is released on every exception class. Error details are static or redacted, and display URIs drop credentials/query. The fragile parts are outside the HTTP path: the Git adapter's per-remote fetch cache lives in a predictable shared temp directory with no ownership or locking protection and honours planted repo-local Git config, the Git cache …

Strengths:
- Authority model is airtight in practice: every dispatchable remote target is built only from a StoreAuthorityUseProof via factory-token constructors, the prepared handle is single-use and sealed to the request's ContextQueries instance, and registered local stores fail closed by design.
- Outbound HTTP policy covers the full SSRF checklist (scheme, userinfo, IDNA, local names, IPv4/IPv6 special ranges including IPv4-mapped/6to4/Teredo/NAT64, mixed answers, pinned connect with getpeername verification, TLS SNI/hostname on the logical name, per-hop redirect reauthorisation, no proxy …
- One monotonic deadline per logical operation is threaded through DNS, socket, TLS, header parsing, body streaming and hashing, with drip-feed tests for headers and body; rclone/Git use an equivalent ProcessDeadline across all subcommands.
- Byte limits are enforced at three layers (route/pydantic, ArtifactContentBounds, _HashCollector with max_total) and the sanitize_artifact_resolution_result postcondition independently re-derives size/truncation and re-hashes untruncated content before serialization.
- Registered HTTP prefix confinement is structural (decoded-once PortableStorePath components, NFKC checks, ';' and '//' rejection, raw Location validated before urljoin) with an extensive negative-case matrix in tests/test_registered_http_prefix.py.
- Error handling is fail-closed with static, path/URL-free details; unresolved HTTP results redact the locator and verified/drifted results strip userinfo, query and fragment.
- Admission middleware ordering (security headers > auth > admission > DB session > route) matches the design, and lease release is tested for the full exception hierarchy.

### Authentication and tokens (`auth`)

The auth area is cleanly layered: `auth.py` holds pure services (PBKDF2 password hashing, HS256 JWT issue/verify, hashed-at-rest invitation/device/PAT secrets with prefixes), `app_parts/middleware.py` resolves the bearer credential into an immutable `AuthContext` (user id, role, principal type) on `request.state`, routes read it via `routes/shared.actor_from_request`, and services receive `actor=` explicitly and consult `ProjectAuthorizationPolicy` for project/group membership. Middleware order (security headers → auth → store-health admission → artifact admission → DB session) is correct: unauthenticated requests never open a DB session and the admission gates see an authenticated actor. Timing-safe comparisons are used everywhere they matter (JWT signature, bootstrap token, MCP inbound token), random secrets are 256-bit and only their SHA-256 is stored, invitation and enrollment claims are atomic conditional UPDATEs with good concurrency tests (including a Postgres integration test), and the PAT scope/role policy is fail-closed with thorough unit coverage. The insecure default secret is rejected whenever auth is enabled and auth-disabled mode is confined to `environment=local`. What is fragile: credential lifecycle after issuance. Roles are re-read from the DB for sessions and devices but frozen at issuance for PATs, session JWTs have no revocation or absolute lifetime, and there is no admin-side visibility or revocation of other users' PATs/devices, so demotion or password reset does not actually remove access. Path-based policy is evaluated on the un-stripped request path (unlike the admission middlewares), which makes the fail-closed `/auth/*` policies and public-path allowlist root_path-sensitive. The in-process rate limiter is best-effort (documented as such) but is keyed on attacker-controlled strings with no eviction, and everything peer-IP based (rate keys, local-peer disclosure, HSTS) assumes uvicorn terminates the client connection while the documented topology puts a reverse proxy in front with no trusted-proxy configuration. Actor threading into services is consistent, but principal-type checks are duplicated ad hoc per route (`is_device`/`is_service`) rather than enforced centrally, which is exactly what saved `/auth/tokens` and `/auth/devices` …

Strengths:
- Bearer credentials are dispatched by prefix (ldev_/lpat_/JWT) into a single immutable AuthContext with an explicit principal type; `is_interactive` is an allow-list so new principal types are fail-closed at the accept/commit gates.
- Secrets (invitation, enrollment offer, device, PAT) are 256-bit urlsafe random values stored only as SHA-256 hashes with unique constraints; the raw secret is returned exactly once.
- Invitation acceptance and enrollment consumption are atomic conditional UPDATEs inside one transaction, with user creation rolled back on a lost claim; concurrency tests exist for SQLite and Postgres.
- JWT verification checks the HMAC signature (constant-time) before parsing claims and ignores the embedded role in favour of the live user role; the bootstrap token and MCP inbound token also use compare_digest.
- PAT policy is fail-closed on unknown scopes, caps the granted role at the issuer's role, blocks /auth/* for service principals, and has broad unit and HTTP tests including near-miss paths and the batch_run_due scheduler scope.
- Configuration validation rejects placeholder auth secrets whenever auth is enabled and forbids disabling auth outside environment=local; the Docker entrypoint generates and persists strong secrets.
- Middleware ordering keeps DB sessions closed for unauthenticated requests and applies security headers to auth error responses; ActorAdmission is lock-protected with idempotent lease release and validated limits.
- Bootstrap-token disclosure is gated on the transport peer rather than the Host header, with a regression test for Host spoofing.

### CLI and operational scripts (`cli-ops`)

This area is the operational shell around the layered app: `lab_tracker.cli` is a thin argparse front that composes Alembic, the backup module, `LabTrackerAPI.request_scope` and the consumer-repo scaffolder; `demo_seed` correctly goes through the public API facade rather than the ORM; `dolt_mirror` reads through SQLAlchemy metadata and shells out to a fixed argv (no shell), and `repo_bridge` is a pure, well-documented translation layer whose identity contract with the CI script is pinned by a test. The scheduler installers are a genuinely good design: all quoting-sensitive logic (cron line construction, fail-closed crontab merge, plist rendering, JSON secrets file with O_NOFOLLOW, URL redaction) lives in `scripts/scheduler_install.py` and is exercised by adversarial tests, and the shell adapters never interpolate secrets. The fragile parts are the edges where policy is duplicated instead of shared: `serve-lan.sh` re-implements the non-loopback auth guard from `cli.serve_app` and gets it narrower (only 0.0.0.0/::), the Windows scheduler path re-implements the trigger without the credential-persistence half, and the managed-block upsert in `cli.py` tolerates unpaired markers while `lab_tracker_client.hooks` refuses them. `seed-demo` and the Dolt mirror both lack any awareness of deployment mode (environment/auth), which matters now that hosted and multi-tenant deployments are supported. Test coverage is strong for the Python core (scheduler_install, setup CLI, repo bridge) but thin for the shell/PowerShell/launcher adapters, which are only string-asserted; none of the PowerShell scripts can be executed in CI. Documentation is mostly accurate to the current command surface (I found no documented `lt`/`lab-tracker` subcommand that no longer exists), with drift confined to environment-dependent details.

Strengths:
- scheduler_install.py centralises every injection-sensitive operation (cron field validation with an explicit forbidden-byte set, strict base-URL charset, fail-closed crontab merge, plistlib-escaped plist, JSON secrets with O_NOFOLLOW + 0600) and is covered by adversarial unit and end-to-end tests …
- daily-review-run-due.sh builds the login body with json.dumps and reads secrets structurally; nothing is ever sourced or eval'd, and secrets never appear in the crontab or plist.
- restore_sqlite_backup is careful: read-only URI opens, integrity checks on both sides, advisory ProcessLock coordination with the running server, temp-file + atomic replace, sidecar cleanup, and --force required to overwrite.
- dolt_mirror invokes dolt with a fixed argv list (no shell), uses a deterministic PK-ordered export inside one transaction, distinguishes NULL from empty string, and tests pin the create/replace/add/commit sequence.
- demo_seed goes through the LabTrackerAPI facade so it respects the same invariants as real writes, is idempotent by default, and returns a structured summary.
- repo_bridge is pure and side-effect free, with a contract test that keeps the client adapter and the stdlib CI script's remote normalisation byte-identical.
- The consumer-scaffold code has dry-run previews, unified diffs, and backup copies for scaffold files, and update never touches lt_ids.json.
- create-analysis-graph-draft.py validates the base URL, refuses credentials/query in the URL, bounds diff size, and never uses a shell for git.
