### Python client package (lt) (`client-package`)

The consumer package is a clean HTTP client layered on the server's public API: `transport.py` owns wire mechanics (surface header, one 401 retry, streaming uploads with a local 100 MB preflight), `client.py` owns typed validation and idempotent get-or-create/evidence helpers, and the four capture adapters (watch, repo, hpc, git snapshot) share one durable outbox implementation (`outbox.py`) with per-record quarantine, advisory locking and select-before-limit draining. Credential handling on disk is well done (`lt setup connect` writes the profile via a private temp file, 0600/icacls, redacted diffs, explicit `--save-token` consent), TLS verification is never disabled and httpx 0.28 still honours `SSL_CERT_FILE` for private CAs, and duplicate-submission safety is real: content-bearing uploads carry a `client_capture_id`, file imports dedupe against a server-built evidence index, and the server's acquisition-output registration is idempotent by (session, path). The fragile parts are the places where the adapters diverged instead of sharing: there are four copies of `git_context`/`_git_output` with different credential stripping (repo.py strips nothing, git_capture strips only `user:pass`, figure.py uses `normalize_remote`) and a 1-second timeout that turns a slow `git status` into a false 'clean' provenance claim on exactly the HPC filesystems the hpc adapter targets; two hook installers with different quoting/lt-resolution discipline; and a `stale` sync state that is neither terminal nor pruned, so scheduled limited drains can starve. The evidence index is an O(N-notes) client-side scan repeated on every sync, including inline in the post-commit hook, which will not scale with a project's note count. Error surfacing is mostly good (actionable stderr diagnostics for hook syncs, JSON payload exit codes) but a few messages hide the root cause (profile token blamed on an env var, one-shot figure warnings). The MATLAB package is a faithful, Python-free mirror of the figure-capture contract, though it is only source-contract tested in CI. Overall the area fits the layered design well (nothing bypasses the API), and most defects are consistency/hardening gaps rather than architectural problems.

Strengths:
- Shared outbox drain (`outbox.py`) with per-record quarantine + `.error` sidecars, unique-temp atomic writes, advisory lock, and limit applied only to actionable records; well covered by tests/test_outbox.py.
- Connection profile persistence is consent-gated and hardened: private temp file proven 0600/icacls before credential bytes are written, atomic replace, tokens redacted from all diffs, `--save-token` as a separate consent.
- Streaming multipart uploads with a local size preflight and per-attempt file reopen on 401 retry; 100 MB cap mirrored client-side (tests/test_transport.py).
- Idempotency designed end to end: deterministic `client_capture_id`s for git/hpc/repo evidence, server-built evidence index dedupe for file imports, idempotent server-side acquisition-output registration, and tests for the persisted-upload-then-client-failure retry case.
- Repo hook installer single-quotes shell values, refuses non-sh interpreters, inserts its block before a foreign hook body, and never blocks a commit; the graph-draft hook installer carries legacy bindings forward and refuses unpaired markers.
- Crontab management preserves the user's lines verbatim and refuses to rewrite from an unknown state; schtasks failures are surfaced rather than reported as 'absent'.
- TLS verification is never disabled anywhere in the client or MATLAB package; httpx's default context honours `SSL_CERT_FILE`/`SSL_CERT_DIR` for private CAs.
- Actionable stderr diagnostics for failed hook syncs (cause, queue size, exact drain command) and `lt outbox status/sync` work without a watch.json.

### Concurrency and performance sweep (`concurrency-perf`)

The request path is cleanly layered and, on the async/blocking axis, essentially correct: every route handler is a plain def (run in Starlette's threadpool), the async middlewares only touch the database through run_in_threadpool (token verification, get_user_by_id, request-scope commit/rollback/close), the request-scoped SQLAlchemy session is created lazily so static and public requests never open a connection, and middleware ordering puts auth outside the DB-session scope so unauthenticated requests never build a repository. The catalog read model (CatalogQueries -> repository query_* with SQL LIMIT/OFFSET, project_ids IN (...), and batched child-row hydration via the *_map helpers) fixed the whole-table pagination class the previous review reported for the core entity lists, and graph_query.py, portfolio_query.py and decision_context_query.py are bounded, one-round-trip-per-branch SQL with a scale test (tests/test_graph_query_scale.py) that pins statement counts. Index coverage in db_models.py matches the filters and orderings the repositories actually use (project+created_at composites, membership lookups, claimable-run and outbox claim indexes, capture-key uniques). Background work is well designed: asyncio tasks own the graph-draft worker, scheduler and SMTP outbox loops, each tick runs in asyncio.to_thread with its own session, exceptions are logged and back off, and claims are fenced with conditional UPDATEs plus leases so multi-process or crashed workers recover; SQLite gets WAL, busy_timeout=5000, legacy transaction control (reads hold no lock) and explicit BEGIN IMMEDIATE / project-row write fences before reservation reads, and provider calls happen after a committed claim checkpoint so no transaction or pooled connection is held across an LLM or SMTP round trip. The fragile spots are the endpoints that never migrated to the paged read model: /batches and /batches/runs (hit by the app shell's PendingBatchBanner on every load) still load every batch change set with all operations across all projects and then paginate and authorize per item in Python, /goals does per-goal authorization queries over an unbounded list, and a few admin or provenance paths read whole tables into memory. The one shared in-memory structure without a bound is the …

Strengths:
- All HTTP handlers are sync def and every DB call in async middleware is wrapped in run_in_threadpool; no event-loop blocking SQLAlchemy calls were found.
- Middleware order (security headers > auth > admission > db session) means unauthenticated requests never create a repository, and the request scope commits/rolls back/closes in the threadpool with BaseException-safe cleanup.
- Core entity list endpoints push project scoping, filters, ordering and LIMIT/OFFSET into SQL and hydrate child rows with batched IN queries (notes_from_rows, claims_from_rows, questions_from_rows, etc.), eliminating the earlier whole-table pagination for those routes.
- graph_query.py and portfolio_query.py are bounded aggregate SQL (union_all branches, LIMIT+1 has_more, frontier IN predicates, window-function group paging) and tests/test_graph_query_scale.py pins statement counts at 25k nodes.
- Background loops (graph-draft worker, scheduler, review-email outbox) run each tick in asyncio.to_thread with a fresh session, catch and log exceptions with backoff, and use conditional-UPDATE claims with leases and SKIP LOCKED on Postgres so crashes and multi-process deployments recover safely.
- SQLite contention is handled deliberately: WAL, busy_timeout=5000, LEGACY_TRANSACTION_CONTROL so authorization reads take no snapshot, and explicit write fences (BEGIN IMMEDIATE / project-row UPDATE) before reservation reads; provider calls occur after a committed claim checkpoint so no pooled …
- Shared caches (CachedStoreHealthProbe, ActorAdmission) are lock-protected and bounded; token last_used_at writes are throttled to once per 5 minutes so PAT/device auth does not turn every request into a write.
- Index declarations in db_models.py are mirrored 1:1 by Alembic migrations and cover the membership, claimable-run, outbox-claim, note-target and goal-link lookups the repositories use.

### Failure-mode sweep (`config-failure-modes`)

The backend is unusually disciplined for a lab tool: Settings is a single pydantic model with strict validators and no boolean coercion tricks, there are no tz-naive datetimes or mutable default arguments anywhere in src, the request transaction boundary (LabTrackerRequestScope.complete_response commits only on <400 and rolls back on any exception or 4xx) is sound given Starlette's exception-handler ordering, file/note storage writes are atomic with cleanup on BaseException, and the outbound-HTTP, subprocess and store-health modules consistently redact provider errors and preserve interrupts. Most broad `except Exception` sites are translate-and-reraise, per-record client outbox boundaries, or background-worker ticks that log with traceback, which is acceptable. The material violations of the fail-loud standard cluster at the composition edges rather than in the domain: (1) `db.py` builds Settings and an engine at import time via an unused `SessionLocal`, and `lab_tracker/__init__.py` eagerly imports the whole API, so client, MCP and the documented external review-email worker crash on server-config validation they do not need — that profile is broken as shipped; (2) the app performs no schema/revision check at startup and downgrades the only DB touch to a warning while `/health` (used by every orchestrator healthcheck) never touches the DB; (3) the in-memory rate limiter never evicts and is keyed per PAT hash, so it neither bounds memory nor limits token spraying; (4) a second, undocumented env-reading config path in `registry_from_env` silently discards invalid git-cache limits; (5) the MCP target-safety probe swallows unreachable/5xx targets. Secure-by-default is weakened by public viewer registration defaulting on in the hosted templates. Typing is strong where the mypy list reaches (resolvers, subprocess, graph-draft pipeline) but the auth/authorization/route/repository layers are outside strict checking (29 errors when checked). Overall the layered design (routes -> handlers -> services -> repository) is respected; the fragility is concentrated in startup/import ordering and a handful of silent fallbacks rather than in business logic.

Strengths:
- Settings validation is strict and centralized: insecure secret placeholders rejected when auth is enabled, non-local environments cannot disable auth, resolver/store-health limits bounded with explicit maxima, boolean-typed inputs rejected for numeric fields.
- No timezone-naive datetime usage, no mutable default arguments, and no try/except-guarded imports in the server package (only tomllib/fcntl/msvcrt platform shims in the client).
- Request transaction boundary is correct: LabTrackerRequestScope commits only for <400 responses, rolls back on 4xx and on any exception, and deferred after-commit/after-rollback actions are drained without changing the decided outcome.
- File and note storage writes are atomic (temp file + fsync + os.replace) with cleanup on BaseException; backup/restore uses read-only URIs, integrity checks, an advisory process lock and an exclusive-transaction secondary guard.
- Security boundaries fail closed: unknown PAT scopes are denied, device/service principals use allow-lists, invitation and enrollment consumption use conditional UPDATE ... WHERE for single-use semantics, and outbound HTTP/subprocess/store-health code redacts provider details and preserves …
- Background workers and the client outbox use explicit per-tick / per-record recovery boundaries that log with traceback or surface errors in JSON rather than aborting the whole loop.
- The deployment probe verifies migration heads, users/admin presence and storage writability inside the release container, and docker-entrypoint.sh retries `alembic upgrade head` and exits non-zero on failure.

### Data integrity sweep (`data-integrity`)

The write path is layered cleanly: HTTP middleware owns one request transaction (LabTrackerRequestScope), while direct/background callers get an explicit ApplicationTransaction with nested-join semantics, after-commit/after-rollback hooks, and savepoint-based recoverable units for idempotency-key collisions; the atomicity of composite commands (analysis commit, graph-draft commit, evidence bundles, project+owner membership) is tested and holds. Idempotency is consistently scoped (projects per creator, questions/notes per project, evidence bundles per project+creator, batch runs per stable key) and backed by real unique constraints with IntegrityError recovery rather than Python-only checks. Blob writes are correctly ordered after row locks with rollback/commit cleanup hooks, and Postgres concurrency around datasets, files, experiments, question DAGs and member onboarding is covered by real advisory locks plus dedicated Postgres tests. Timestamps are uniformly aware-UTC via UtcDateTime with tz-required inputs where they matter. The fragile part is deletion semantics: the schema mixes CASCADE, SET NULL and FK-less GUID references (manifest_source_session_id, note_targets.entity_id, provenance/exploration targets), and the application guards that are supposed to protect provenance are uneven — datasets/questions/sessions/notes have partial guards, claims have none — so contributor-level deletes can silently strip evidence, supersession history, pivot invalidations and manifest references. A second gap is that only some check-then-write invariants got the advisory-lock treatment (claim-edge acyclicity and dataset-delete-vs-claim-create did not), and every lock helper is a no-op on SQLite even though the app serves concurrent requests there. Offboarding is a contract-drift hotspot: ownership reassignment covers a hard-coded table list that has not kept up with newer attributed tables. Migration hygiene is good (preflight checks, merge revisions, single head), with the caveat that SQLite runs without transactional DDL and only the newest revisions take the writer fence.

Strengths:
- ApplicationTransaction/RepositoryUnitOfWork give one commit decision per top-level command with nested joins, deferred after-commit/after-rollback actions and reusable state; tests/test_application_transaction_atomicity.py covers direct, HTTP and Postgres paths.
- Idempotency keys are enforced by real unique constraints (uq_projects_creator_client_capture, uq_questions_project_client_capture, uq_notes_project_client_capture, uq_evidence_bundles_project_creator_key, batch_key) and the services recover IntegrityError via savepoints without rolling back …
- Blob lifecycle is ordered correctly: row/advisory locks are taken before store_stream, orphan blobs are removed on rollback, old blobs only after commit; visualization uploads add compare-and-set preconditions.
- Postgres advisory-lock plans (project DAG, session acquisition, experiment, dataset-file shared/exclusive) have documented canonical ordering to avoid deadlocks and are exercised by real Postgres race tests with pg_blocking_pids.
- Migrations preflight data before adding constraints (0054, 0056, 0058), never clamp scientific values, and the codebase enforces a single Alembic head; SQLite FK pragma is set on every connection in production engines.
- All stored timestamps go through UtcDateTime and inputs that matter (observed_at) require a timezone; graph-draft claim/lease tokens use compare-and-set updates with rowcount checks.

### ORM models and migrations (`db-models-migrations`)

The persistence layer is cleanly layered: domain pydantic models cross into SQLAlchemy 2 declarative models through explicit mapper functions, and the three TypeDecorators (GUID, EnumType, UtcDateTime) push UUID/enum/UTC normalisation into the column types so the mappers are mostly mechanical and byte-compatible with the old string storage. Cascades in the model are deliberate and consistent (project-owned data CASCADE, attribution SET NULL, audit/supervision RESTRICT), CHECK constraints for claim confidence, outbox state, data-store scope and authority-binding formats reach both SQLite and Postgres, and the migration chain is a single head with the two historical forks correctly merged. The fragile part is the SQLite migration runtime: Alembic's batch rebuilds interact badly with the FK pragma now installed on Alembic's engine (July 2026), and the per-migration pragma toggles are no-ops on the re-enable side, so whether `alembic upgrade head` preserves or destroys child rows depends on the revision the database happens to be stamped at; I reproduced silent deletion of graph_change_operations and change_set_id backlinks for a 0056-0060 -> head upgrade, plus goal_links/claim junction losses and outright upgrade failure for older stamps. SQLite migrations are also non-atomic under pysqlite's legacy transaction mode, so a failure leaves half-applied DDL. Separately, the ORM metadata has drifted from the migrated schema (25 NOT NULL promises the DB does not enforce, one missing FK, 7 unmodelled indexes) with no parity test to catch it, and the mappers absorb the gap with `or {}` fallbacks. Postgres paths look sound from code reading (dialect-guarded backfills, LOCK TABLE fencing, widened alembic_version), but I could not execute them here.

Strengths:
- TypeDecorators (GUID/EnumType/UtcDateTime) are liberal on input, typed on output, storage-identical, cache-key safe, and covered by real round-trip tests including NULL and naive-datetime handling.
- Enum vocabularies are validated at bind and load time (EnumType raises on unknown values), so corrupt enum strings fail loudly rather than surfacing as None.
- Data-changing migrations (0054, 0056, 0058) run preflight checks with diagnostic samples and refuse to clamp or rewrite scientific values; 0028 backfill guards against unknown/local user ids.
- ON DELETE semantics are consistent across the model: CASCADE for project-owned data and junctions, SET NULL for attribution and change-set backlinks, RESTRICT for audit and supervision edges; note deletion is guarded against dropping change sets (_ensure_note_can_be_deleted).
- Revision chain integrity is enforced (single-head test, upgrade-every-revision test, head->0012->0007->base->head round trip on SQLite and Postgres), and revision IDs are treated as immutable per CLAUDE.md.
- Alembic runs SQLite per-migration transactions (transactional_ddl=False) and widens alembic_version on Postgres before long revision ids appear (0015).

### Decision context and graph queries (`decision-context`)

This area is the read-only "assistant context" layer: decision context (a small package split into constants/types/query/selection/builders/use_case), the SQL-backed GraphQueryService (overview, search, bounded neighborhood), the full-project graph projection with Mermaid export, the portfolio summary read model, next-question ranking, presentation-graph manifests, and signed review links. It fits the layered design well: routes call ContextQueries in application/, which performs the project-read authorization (get_project_for_read / require_project_read / accessible_project_ids) before delegating to pure or repository-backed readers; the decision-context reader takes an explicit accessible_project_ids set and every repository query fails closed on an empty set (verified in core.py, notes.py, datasets.py, analyses.py, sessions.py, and project_ids_with_search_matches), and graph hydration is always joined to the requested project so cross-project anchors and neighbours are indistinguishable from missing ones. I found no cross-tenant exposure. Ordering is deterministic throughout (created_at/updated_at with id tie-breakers, type-order CASE expressions, sorted frontiers), and the neighborhood fetch-limit arithmetic guarantees that any SQL-level cut is surfaced as node_limit_reached or edge_limit_reached. What is fragile is the glue around these cores: the decision-context project resolution depends on a 500-row oldest-first project window and fails with a misleading error beyond it; the MCP tool surface drifted from the HTTP contract (progress_review scoping parameters are missing); next_questions is built on client-side list stitching capped at 200 rows with no truncation signal and treats rejected claims as answers; and the product spec describes ranking and scope behaviour that was never implemented. The full project-graph projection is the one unbounded read left in the area. Tests are strong on determinism, opacity of 404s, truncation flags, unicode search, and the crypto properties of review links, but thin on next_questions semantics, on the MCP decision-context parameter set, and on project-graph scaling.

Strengths:
- Repository queries treat an empty accessible-project set as 'return nothing' (core.py:87, notes.py:539, datasets.py:118, analyses.py:131/302/548, sessions.py:52, repository.py:1125), so the decision-context reader is fail-closed for users with no memberships.
- GraphQueryService scopes every hydration and edge branch to the requested project and raises the same opaque NotFoundError for missing, cross-project, or inaccessible anchors (graph_query.py:319-325, 649-720); tests assert this (test_graph_neighborhood_project_mismatch_is_opaque).
- Neighborhood traversal is genuinely bounded and deterministic: fetch_limit = min(701, max_edges - len(edges) + max_nodes + 201) plus stable multi-key ordering guarantees that any SQL truncation is reported as node_limit_reached/edge_limit_reached, and external artifacts are kept as leaves.
- Search ranking (exact id/title > prefix > substring, then updated_at desc, type order, id) is computed in SQL with escaped LIKE patterns; wildcard injection is covered by the '%_' test.
- Decision-context reads are batch-hydrated (notes_from_rows/visualizations_from_rows build target/claim/dataset maps in one query each), so there is no N+1 in the assembled payload; notes are compacted with explicit truncated_fields metadata.
- review_links.py is a careful HMAC design: domain-separated signing context, constant-time compare, canonical base64url check, expiry, exact payload key set, recipient/delivery binding, and the token confers no authority by itself; tests cover tampering, legacy signature, malformed tokens, and …
- Portfolio summary uses a window-function page over groups and per-page aggregate queries (counts, last activity, owners, gaps) rather than loading entities, and short-circuits on an empty accessible set.
- Presentation-graph manifests are validated against the project-graph relationship vocabulary by an AST-based test, keeping slides and backend tokens aligned.

### Packaging, Docker and CI (`deploy-packaging`)

The packaging/deployment area is well structured: a single setuptools-built wheel with explicit package-data for the frontend bundle and Alembic tree (verified by wheel-install tests that run migrations and serve PWA assets from the extracted wheel), a slim non-root Docker image whose entrypoint provisions runtime secrets with 0600 files and applies migrations before exec, a Render blueprint that reuses the same entrypoint (RENDER_EXTERNAL_URL fallback, generated secrets), and a genuinely careful dedicated-instance release template (env-file permission gates, placeholder rejection, paused-app checkpoint, atomic `.partial` rename, manifest verification, disposable restore, git-archive build with revision label check, in-container probe, image-only rollback with health wait, mkdir-based release lock). The fragile parts are the seams between these pieces rather than the pieces themselves: the root docker-compose.yml couples an optional MCP service to the mandatory app path via `:?` interpolation so the primary documented commands fail without MCP tokens; the mcp service reuses the app image but inherits an authenticated-only HEALTHCHECK and starts via `uv run`, which re-syncs the immutable venv at runtime; and dependency hygiene relies entirely on `--frozen`, which let a pyproject/lock mismatch and an over-broad `mcp<3` bound (verified to break the MCP import) land on main. Tests for the shell scripts are string-presence assertions, so they document intent but cannot catch runtime behaviour such as the pg_isready/temp-server race in restore-smoke. CI is broad on Python/OS matrices but never builds or runs the container and never validates the lockfile, which is exactly where the confirmed defects live. Secrets handling is otherwise sound: no credentials are committed, examples use placeholders that release.sh rejects, .dockerignore/.gitignore cover the env files and backups, and the provider key is injected as a single variable.

Strengths:
- Wheel contents are guarded by tests that diff package-data patterns against the on-disk frontend and alembic trees and then install the built wheel to run migrations and serve PWA assets (tests/test_packaging.py).
- Docker image runs as a dedicated system user, has a HEALTHCHECK, bounded migration retries, and 0600 runtime secrets generated under umask 077; Render config reuses the same entrypoint with platform-generated secrets and RENDER_EXTERNAL_URL fallback.
- Dedicated-instance release workflow is defensive end to end: env-file mode checks, placeholder/hostname/URL-safety gates, clean-and-pushed worktree requirement, paused-app coherent backup with atomic .partial rename and SHA-256 manifest, disposable restore proving the backup, revision-labelled …
- Shared-provider overlay uses `build: !reset null` plus required env files so a pinned instance cannot be rebuilt from the checkout, and a real `docker compose config` regression test verifies the merged model.
- Config normalises `postgres://` and `postgresql://` to the psycopg driver, so Render's fromDatabase connection string works without operator edits.
- Doc-parity test scans Dockerfile/compose/render.yaml for LAB_TRACKER_* variables so undocumented settings cannot appear in deployment files.

### Dependency and supply-chain sweep (`deps-supply-chain`)

The dependency/tooling layer is deliberately reproducible at its core: Python deps are locked in uv.lock with hashes and resolution markers for 3.10-3.15, the Dockerfile installs with `uv sync --frozen --no-dev --no-editable` as a non-root user, npm uses lockfileVersion 3 with `npm ci`, CI pins Python (3.11 / 3.10) and Node (22), and the committed frontend bundle, generated OpenAPI types and skill reference are all drift-checked in CI. The 3.10 compatibility claim is real (full suite runs on 3.10; only the tomli fallback is an undeclared transitive dependency), no 3.11+ syntax or stdlib APIs are used, and the pyproject-vs-lock comparison shows the mcp specifier as the only declared-range drift. The fragile parts sit at the seams between tools: Dependabot's `pip` ecosystem edits pyproject.toml without touching uv.lock (that is exactly how the known mcp drift landed), and CI combines `uv sync --frozen` with plain `uv run`, so lock staleness is never detected and the lock is silently re-resolved in the workspace. The same `uv run` semantics leak into runtime: the compose `mcp` service (inherited by the shared-provider overlay) launches with `uv run`, which re-locks and rebuilds the project editable at every container start, defeating the frozen image and failing offline. The `npm audit --audit-level=high` hard gate is currently red at HEAD on a babel/browserslist advisory reachable only through eslint-plugin-react-hooks, blocking unrelated PRs. Docs consistently teach `uv pip install -e` + plain `uv run` and never mention `uv sync`/`--frozen`/`uv lock --check`, and there is no pre-commit config, so contributors have no guard either. Minor items: uv itself is the one unpinned tool in the chain, and the mcp container runs the app entrypoint (migrations against an orphan SQLite, unused secrets). Fixes are small and local (dependabot ecosystem, a `uv lock --check` CI step, `python -m` in compose, audit scoping, doc alignment) and would make the layer match the project's fail-loud/config-driven standard.

Strengths:
- uv.lock is a full hashed universal lock (revision 3, markers for 3.10 through 3.15) and the Dockerfile consumes it with `uv sync --frozen --no-dev --no-editable --compile-bytecode`, running as a dedicated non-root user with a real HEALTHCHECK against /health.
- CI drift gates are unusually thorough: committed frontend bundle (`git diff --exit-code`), generated OpenAPI TypeScript declarations (`--check`), skill API reference (`--check`), tsc contract typecheck, plus a dedicated Python 3.10 job that runs the full suite and platform-specific …
- package.json and package-lock.json root entries match exactly; `npm ci --dry-run` resolves cleanly; Node engine requirement (>=22) matches the CI runtime.
- No Python 3.11+ syntax or stdlib APIs are used in src/tests/scripts; ruff `target-version = py310` and mypy `python_version = 3.10` back the declared minimum, and tomllib/tomli fallbacks are consistent.
- Deployment overlays (dedicated-instance, shared-provider) pin an immutable image, `!reset null` the build section, and have tests (`test_compose_merge_has_pinned_images_and_no_builds`, release.sh dirty-worktree refusal, placeholder checks) that enforce the fail-closed posture.
- docker-entrypoint.sh fails loudly after a bounded migration retry budget, persists generated secrets with umask 077, and is covered by tests/test_packaging.py; the Dockerfile COPY sources are asserted to exist in the build context.
- CI workflow avoids duplicate push+PR runs via `push: branches: [main]` plus a concurrency group, uses no secrets or `pull_request_target`, so fork PRs run the same jobs safely.

### Documentation parity (`docs-drift`)

The documentation surface is unusually disciplined for a research tool: docs/retained-v1-surface.md is treated as the authority and other pages defer to it explicitly, configuration.md is guarded by a parity test against Settings fields, the two SKILL.md files are generated/drift-tested against package code (setup guide, MCP tool list, OpenAPI-derived field reference), and the layered design described in internal-boundaries.md matches what I found in routes/app_parts/application/services. Endpoints, CLI verbs and flags cited in the docs I checked (serve/seed-demo/doctor/backup/restore/update, lt watch/hpc/repo/setup/project/auth, accept-all, notes archive, publication-readiness, provenance-links, usage-events, /terms, run-due, project-default settings, readiness/metrics auth) all exist and behave as described; the vision doc's security claims (auth cannot be disabled outside local, placeholder secrets rejected, non-loopback serve refused with auth off) are implemented in config.py, cli.py and serve-lan.sh. The fragile part is everything the parity test does not see: dependency ranges vs. the lockfile (the mcp 2.x bump breaks every documented non-lockfile install and the MCP server, while CI stays green on `uv sync --frozen`), prose-level setup sequences (the first-admin non-Docker flow omits the mandatory secret), the dual-use `.env`/`.env.example` that is read by both Compose interpolation and pydantic Settings, and non-Settings environment variables read directly via os.getenv in the client, entrypoint and scripts. Agent-facing instruction files have drifted from each other: AGENTS.md carries an imperative push-at-all-costs section that contradicts the conservative profile in both AGENTS.md and CLAUDE.md, and the skill/MCP docs still lead with the deprecated username/password auth that `lt auth doctor` flags. Long-form design docs (vision, build-vs-buy, loud-alignment-plan, curation-states, review-and-commit-model) are clearly dated and status-labelled, which keeps them from masquerading as current requirements; only a few sentences (offline capture 'deferred', secret 'allowed only in local') are stale. Overall the doc-to-code contract is strong where a test enforces it and leaky where it relies on prose.

Strengths:
- docs/retained-v1-surface.md is a real single source of truth: setup.md, configuration.md, vision.md and the skill all explicitly state that it wins on conflict, and its listed endpoints/CLI verbs exist in code.

- tests/test_configuration_doc_parity.py enforces both directions between Settings fields and configuration.md bullets, so server-side settings cannot be added or renamed undocumented.

- Design and vision documents are dated and status-labelled (shipped/designed/reserved, 'Status: implemented'), and the loud-alignment plan records that all phases shipped, so historical design docs are not easily mistaken for current requirements.
- Deployment assets are consistent with the docs: Dockerfile uses `uv sync --frozen`, the entrypoint generates the auth secret and bootstrap token as documented, render.yaml opts into `first_run` disclosure exactly as one-click-cloud-deploy.md says.

### Domain models and schemas (`domain-models`)

The domain layer is cleanly separated: models.py holds Pydantic domain models (from_attributes) that double as response payloads, schemas.py holds explicit request models with extra='forbid' and a well-designed PatchRequestModel that distinguishes omission from explicit null (exhaustively tested in tests/test_request_schema_validation.py), and errors.py provides a small taxonomy that routes/errors.py maps to HTTP codes. Claim confidence is enforced consistently at four layers (request schema, domain model with validate_assignment, service _ensure_claim_confidence, and a DB CheckConstraint), and the supported-claim evidence invariant is enforced on create, update, and analysis deletion. Status transitions for questions, datasets, sessions, analyses and claims live in one table in services/shared.py with a shared enforcement helper and tests. The vocabulary registry is a good pattern: concept schemes are tested against the enums, and the JSON-LD context and /terms page are generated from the same data. The fragile parts are where the layers disagree: notes and goals have no transition tables at all, so the documented archive-with-reason invariant is bypassable through ordinary status PATCH/POST; the question transition table admits 'superseded' without the refactor command that gives it meaning; request schemas do not carry the VARCHAR lengths the ORM declares, so SQLite and Postgres behave differently; nested domain models reused inside requests silently ignore unknown keys; request datetimes are not required to be timezone-aware, producing a reproducible 500 on supervision edges; and the single AuthError collapses authentication and authorization into HTTP 401, which the CLI and MCP clients interpret as credential rejection. Overall the area is well engineered and heavily tested for the invariants it names, but several invariants are enforced only on the 'happy' command path and not in the generic update surface.

Strengths:
- PatchRequestModel + non_nullable_fields cleanly separates omitted, null and valued PATCH fields, and a parametrized test covers every schema/field pair including OpenAPI nullability.
- ClaimConfidence is one Annotated type shared by request schemas, ClaimInput and Claim (validate_assignment) and backed by a DB CheckConstraint; tests cover NaN/inf and boundary values end to end.
- Status transition tables are centralized in services/shared.py with a single _ensure_status_transition helper and message-level tests; terminal_reason_for_status/for_patch enforce reasons for abandoned/rejected states.
- ExternalArtifactReference validates paired store_name/locator, normalizes JSON-safe metadata, and has canonical constructors per store kind; URI validation is applied uniformly in claim, analysis, dataset, evidence-bundle and publication-readiness services.
- Vocabulary registry generates both the JSON-LD @context and the /terms documentation from one Term table, with tests that every emitted lab: IRI resolves and every concept scheme matches its Python enum.
- Goal attributes are validated per goal_type with extra='forbid' models and re-validated in the service when either goal_type or attributes change.
- The HTTP error handler set is ordered by specificity (PayloadTooLarge before Validation, RateLimit before Auth) and hides internal messages for 500s.

### Evidence bundles and provenance (`evidence-provenance`)

This area is a thin, well-layered slice of the codebase: routes delegate to application handlers (context_queries for provenance, evidence_bundles mixin for the bundle command) which call pure builders (provenance.py) or a single composite service (EvidenceBundleService) that reuses the existing per-entity services for every write. The evidence-bundle command is the strongest piece: it normalises and fingerprints the request before touching the database, validates every referenced component through project-scoped loaders that return opaque NotFound errors (no cross-project ID probing), runs all writes inside one request- or application-owned transaction, persists a principal-scoped idempotency row via a real unique constraint, and on a concurrent-insert race performs a full rollback before replaying the winner. The MCP adapter keeps the server as the sole authority (strict payload, snapshot-then-upload with checksum/size/CAS verification, loud attachment-failure errors) and the file upload path verifies declared checksum/size against the stored stream and cleans up blobs on failure. Provenance export is deterministic, uses a single vocabulary registry, satisfies the semantic profile's one-type/classifiedAs contract, and the committed example is regenerated by a test so it cannot drift. The fragile parts are the document-composition builders: build_claim_provenance_document and build_record_export_provenance_document assemble graphs by merging sub-documents with first-writer-wins semantics and then overwrite person nodes, and the claim builder only routes visualizations through supporting analyses, so both silently lose information the handler already fetched (findings 1 and 2). Provenance-link routes are correct on the read/list path but the PATCH route bypasses the opaque-404 policy. provenance_ingestion.py mixes a genuinely used helper (external_artifacts_from_metadata) with unused DVC/DataLad importers and a homegrown YAML subset parser that would violate the fail-loud rule if it were ever wired in. Test coverage for the bundle command (race, rollback, key scoping, revoked access, reserved metadata, replay/conflict) and for the MCP adapter is thorough; coverage of provenance document composition is weaker (single-path route tests, no claim/visualization or …

Strengths:
- EvidenceBundleService: authorization before idempotency lookup, project-scoped component loaders with opaque NotFound (no cross-project ID oracle), single transaction boundary, principal-scoped idempotency key backed by a DB unique constraint, and full rollback plus winner replay on …
- Request fingerprint is built from a canonical, normalised command (sorted IDs, inferred links, normalised manifests and upload intent), so replay vs conflict detection is deterministic and dry-run/commit share identical validation.
- MCP evidence-bundle adapter keeps the server authoritative: strict server payload, private 0600 snapshot of the upload with pre/post size preflight and SHA-256, compare-and-set on the current storage id, verification of the returned asset metadata, and explicit non-rollback attachment-failure …
- Visualization upload path enforces content-length and streaming max_bytes, validates declared checksum/size against the stored bytes, takes a row lock, and registers after-rollback/after-commit blob cleanup.
- Provenance export uses one vocabulary registry, emits both legacy literals and classifiedAs concept IRIs per the semantic profile, honours LAB_TRACKER_BASE_URL for stable @ids, content-negotiates the same document on resource URIs, and regenerates docs/examples/dataset.prov.jsonld from the builder …
- Provenance links are proposal-only (no public create endpoint), status transitions are a fixed state machine, accepted links stamp human acceptance provenance, and only accepted was_derived_from links project into PROV-O.

### File storage and uploads (`file-storage`)

The file/blob area follows the layered design cleanly: routes (dataset_files.py, visualizations.py, notes.py) do only HTTP adaptation, DatasetFileCommands/VisualizationFileCommands in application/file_commands.py own the transaction-scoped logic, and LocalFileStorageBackend/LocalNoteStorage are dumb content-addressed stores keyed by server-generated UUIDs, so client-supplied filenames never touch a filesystem path. Storage writes are write-to-temp + fsync + os.replace with cleanup on failure, streaming size enforcement, SHA-256 computed on the fly, and blob lifecycle is tied to the DB transaction via run_after_rollback/run_after_commit hooks; the route tests cover commit-failure cleanup for both upload and delete, cascade deletion from dataset/analysis/project, conditional (compare-and-set) visualization replacement with integrity verification, and UTF-8 download filenames with an ASCII fallback. Authorization is consistent and opaque (I verified outsiders get identical 404s for dataset file list/download and that file ids cannot be read through another dataset's URL), the content-type allowlist blocks browser-active types, and the global middleware adds nosniff to every response so no download can be sniffed into HTML. The collection manifest module is a good example of a frozen contract: strict path normalization, lowercase SHA-256, 64-bit size bounds and order-independent hashing, all mirrored by tests. The fragile parts are at the edges: request ingestion is not bounded before Starlette spools multipart bodies to disk (the Content-Length pre-check runs too late and is bypassed by chunked encoding), quick-capture writes bytes before authorization, note raw downloads are fully buffered while dataset downloads stream, and the sidecar-metadata write sits outside the atomic cleanup path with no orphan reconciliation anywhere. The server-side AcquisitionOutputWatcher is the weakest module: it duplicates file_watch.py helpers, swallows every API error silently, and its single-pass stability heuristic can register partial checksums; the documented capture path (lt watch) uses the shared file_watch helpers and a 'changed since scan' guard instead, so the two implementations already diverge.

Strengths:
- Storage ids are server-generated UUIDs sharded into fixed directories; user-supplied filenames are metadata only, so path traversal into storage is structurally impossible (verified in file_storage.py/_data_path and note_storage.py/_path_for).
- Temp-file + fsync + os.replace writes with exception cleanup in both backends, and tests that prove no partial files remain after stream errors, oversize rejection, or a failing os.replace.
- Blob lifecycle is bound to the request transaction through run_after_rollback / run_after_commit hooks; route tests force a commit failure and assert the blob count on disk for both upload and delete.
- Visualization asset replacement takes the row lock before any durable write, supports idempotent retries via declared checksum/size, enforces expected_current_storage_id preconditions, and verifies uploaded bytes against declared integrity metadata before committing.
- Content-Disposition builder produces an ASCII fallback plus RFC 5987 filename*, strips path segments and CR/LF, and is covered by a non-ASCII route test; the security-headers middleware applies X-Content-Type-Options: nosniff and X-Frame-Options: DENY globally.
- Upload content-type validation normalizes parameters/case and blocks HTML/SVG/JS/XHTML/PHP types, with tests at both unit and route level for notes, datasets, and visualizations.
- Dataset file reads are project-scoped and opaque: outsider list/download and cross-dataset file-id access all return the same 404 body (verified with a scoped member in a scratch test).

### Frontend build and tests (`frontend-build-tests`)

The frontend build pipeline is in good shape: `scripts/build-frontend.mjs` produces a minified IIFE bundle with no source map, deterministic banner, and a content-hash stamp for app.js/styles.css that it writes into index.html and sw.js; I rebuilt the bundle in a scratch copy of the tree and app.js, index.html, sw.js, styles.css and app.css were all byte-identical to the committed files, and CI enforces this with `git diff --exit-code` after `npm run build:frontend`. The OpenAPI contract generator is deterministic and environment-independent (identical output with auth disabled, auth enabled, and environment=production), its `--check` gate passes, tsc on the contracts tsconfig passes, and ESLint reports zero errors and zero warnings. The main structural limitation is scope rather than defect: `typecheck:frontend` only checks the 11 files listed in tsconfig.frontend-contracts.json (the generated declaration, api/auth-storage/contract and the gateways, plus transitively member-onboarding.js), not features/, hooks/ or app-shell, and the CI step is honestly named 'Typecheck frontend API contracts'; similarly the lint job runs with `--quiet` and react-hooks `exhaustive-deps` is warn-level, so warnings can never gate CI (currently none exist), and `set-state-in-effect` is deliberately off with four suppressed sites. The concrete defects I found cluster in the demo/publishing path and test hygiene: the GitHub Pages demo's 404.html uses relative asset URLs so every nested route is blank (verified live), the live demo is stale and unautomated, the production bundle carries a URL-toggleable fake-data mode, and the service-worker cache key only tracks two of the assets it serves cache-first. Test infrastructure is thoughtful (strict unexpected-fetch mocking, shared fixtures, xyflow mocked globally, generous CI timeouts) but the shared setup does not unstub globals so fetch mocks leak between tests, and the E2E suite has retry-unsafe fixed identifiers and sleep-based negative assertions in its security-relevant checks. The E2E harness itself (disposable SQLite servers, prefix proxy, fake onboarding provider gated behind an explicit env var, port-collision-fails-fast) fits the layered design well and exercises the shell, prefix routing, offline SW and share-target paths …

Strengths:
- Committed bundle is reproducible: a clean rebuild in a scratch copy produced byte-identical app.js/index.html/sw.js, and CI enforces freshness with git diff --exit-code after the build.
- Production build ships no source map, is minified, and tests/test_frontend.py asserts no sourceMappingURL and 404 for app.js.map; dev/watch builds are explicitly kept from dirtying tracked shell files.
- OpenAPI declaration generator is deterministic and independent of runtime settings (identical output under local/auth-disabled, auth-enabled and production configurations); operation-rooted selection means response_model changes trip the gate.
- ESLint 10 flat config with react-hooks v7 recommended rules currently yields zero errors and zero warnings across frontend_src; a custom rule models the classic JSX runtime instead of dragging in the legacy React plugin.
- Frontend unit test harness is strict by default: installFetchMock throws on any unmocked request, fixtures are shared between shell and feature-integration projects, and @xyflow/react is stubbed once in setup.
- E2E suite is well isolated: disposable temp SQLite DBs and storage dirs per server, reuseExistingServer=false so port collisions fail loudly, a real prefix-stripping proxy for non-root deployments, and the deterministic onboarding provider refuses to load unless …
- Security-relevant browser behaviours are covered end-to-end (invite token never appears in request URLs, offline queue records contain no credential-shaped keys, delayed responses cannot mutate a different route).
- Screenshot server guards against path traversal (basename check) and binds to 127.0.0.1 only; demo build strips share_target and skips service-worker registration.

### Frontend core (`frontend-core`)

The frontend shell is a small, well-layered React 19 app: app.jsx boots an ErrorBoundary + service-worker registration, app-shell.jsx composes route-specific feature components around a handful of hooks, and all network access funnels through shared/api.js (strict JSON envelope parsing, typed ContractError on drift, auth-rejection events) and domain gateways that validate representative shapes against OpenAPI-generated typedefs. Routing is a hand-rolled pushState router with UUID-validated params and a deployment-prefix resolver; tokens are kept in memory with a resilient localStorage adapter (shadow + tombstones) that cannot crash the session and does not lose a valid token on transient /auth/me failures. The service worker is deliberately narrow (precache + cache-first for /app/static, navigation fallback to the shell, never caches API responses), the update lifecycle uses a prompt handshake with a legacy auto-activate fallback, and the backend serves sw.js with no-store plus a strict CSP (script-src 'self', frame-ancestors 'none') so the localStorage bearer token has limited XSS exposure (the only dangerouslySetInnerHTML is a server-generated QR SVG built from segno with no user input). Most findings from the June review in this area (IDB transaction hang, 4xx drop policy, missing share-target statuses, unversioned cache) are fixed and now covered by tests. The fragile parts are the identity/session gates that were layered onto the upload queue: the drain guard requires a bearer token, which no auth-disabled deployment has, the share-inbox migration accepts an empty owner, and quarantined records are never surfaced, so the offline-capture story can strand data silently in exactly the modes it exists for. The app-shell wiring also undoes the last-used-project restore that the workspace hook implements and tests in isolation, and the auth-rejection path keys on English error strings that do not match the device-token middleware messages. Share-target handling trusts any same-origin navigation POST, which makes cross-site note injection plausible.

Strengths:
- Strict, fail-loud API contract layer: parseResource/parseCollection reject malformed 2xx envelopes and inconsistent pagination meta; fetchAllPages verifies meta.offset and terminates safely.
- Auth storage adapter (auth-storage.js) handles quota/SecurityError on read, write and remove with shadow values and tombstones, with thorough tests; boot only clears the session on 401/403, not on network errors.
- Service worker never caches authenticated API responses; sw.js is served with no-store and registered with updateViaCache: none; CACHE_VERSION and ?v= tokens are single-sourced by the build script and enforced by service-worker-static.test.js (verified: 14 shared test files, 160 tests pass).
- Upload queue persists an owner identity instead of a bearer token, drains only under the live session token, classifies 401/403 vs retryable 4xx vs permanent 4xx vs 5xx sensibly, uses proper IndexedDB transaction completion, and is tested against fake-indexeddb.
- Strong security headers and CSP applied to /app and download paths (script-src 'self', object-src 'none', frame-ancestors 'none'); the only innerHTML sink is a server-built QR SVG with no attacker-controlled input.
- Invitation tokens are moved to the URL fragment and scrubbed from history before any request is made; enrollment/consume endpoints are public but one-time and 5-minute TTL.

### Frontend features: capture, notes, auth (`frontend-features-a`)

This area is a thin React layer over typed gateways: each feature page (capture, notes, enroll, devices, users, agents, onboarding, member onboarding) owns local UI state and delegates network calls to `shared/gateways/*` (runtime-validated against generated OpenAPI types) or `apiRequest`, while controller hooks (`useMobileCapture`, `useMemberOnboarding`, `useAuthSession`, `useProjectAccess`) hold the workflows. The layering is good: presentational components (`CaptureComposer`, `CaptureContextFields`, `PendingReviewList`) are stateless, the offline queue and share-inbox are isolated modules with injectable storage, and the member-onboarding hook uses request generations plus a (projectId, token) context stamp so stale responses can never overwrite the current project. Auth handling is careful: token persistence goes through a resilient adapter with tombstones, invitation tokens are scrubbed from the URL before requests, session rejection is distinguished from permission 401s by message pattern, and the upload queue is bound to an owner id rather than a persisted bearer token. The one-time PAT display keeps the secret out of the copied shell blocks (hidden-prompt read), and setup commands fail closed when the server does not report an immutable revision. The fragile parts are the capture controller and the note detail card: `useMobileCapture` is a large closure-based state machine with no in-flight guard, ids that outlive success, and no `busy` wiring, and `NoteDetailCard` never refreshes its `note` after mutations, so later PATCHes replay stale metadata over server-written provenance. Local drafts are project-keyed and only cleared on explicit logout, which leaves the session-expiry path uncovered on shared machines. Test coverage is strong for happy paths and several race conditions (member onboarding, project access, auth refresh), but there are no tests for concurrent submits or for what the frontend sends after a server-side mutation changed the resource.

Strengths:
- Typed gateways with runtime validators for every auth/member-onboarding/PAT/device response; malformed 2xx payloads fail loudly instead of leaking nulls into components.
- useMemberOnboarding and useProjectAccess sequence requests (generation counter + context stamp) so late responses for a previous project/token can never overwrite the current state, and the tests cover exactly those races.
- Auth session hardening: resilient storage adapter with tombstones/authoritative shadow, invite token scrubbed from URL before any request, 401 rejection vs permission distinction, proactive refresh with bounded retry, and drafts cleared on explicit logout.
- Agent-access page never embeds the one-time secret in copied commands (hidden-prompt `stty -echo` / `Read-Host -AsSecureString` blocks), caps offered token levels at the issuer's role, and withholds install commands unless the server reports a full 40-hex revision.
- Offline upload queue is owner-bound (no persisted bearer token, legacy ownerless jobs quarantined, cross-user jobs skipped), and the capture path only queues on transport failures, surfacing real 4xx/5xx errors.
- Member-onboarding capture handoff validates `return_to` (must start with /app/), locks the checkpoint note target, and never sends client-authored `member_onboarding_*` metadata; the AI-alignment review serializes per-operation decisions with a pending-command ref.
- useLocalDraft is deliberately offer-only (never auto-applies or auto-submits), guards every storage access, and avoids stamping one project's text under another project's key.

### Frontend features: review, graph, entities (`frontend-features-b`)

The reviewed frontend features follow a consistent hook-as-controller / component-as-view split: data controllers (useGraphDraftWorkflow, useDatasetWorkflow, useAnalysisWorkflow, useProjectWorkspaceData, useSessionDetailData) own fetches and mutations, presentational components (OperationRow, NarrativeReview, DatasetPanel, AnalysisPanel, SessionPanel) receive handlers, and app-shell.jsx wires routes to them. The graph-draft review surface is the strongest part: route identity is enforced (loadGenerationRef, isCurrent), every mutation has a synchronous per-command in-flight guard, bulk accept persists buffered edits first and undo is scoped to ids the client just bulk-accepted while deferring to the server's acceptance_mode, and the tests cover these paths explicitly. Media handling is careful (mic released on unmount and on lost edit rights, object URLs revoked, preview fetches bounded to four concurrent workers and cancelled on signature change), and authorization for drafts is re-derived from the draft's own project membership rather than the dashboard selection. The fragile spots are the places that were written outside that discipline: the batches page and the schedule form apply async results without a stale-response guard (reproduced), VisualizationDetailCard bypasses the shared transport and leaves a rejected download unhandled (reproduced), and access-lookups silently degrade to read-only on error. Performance is unbounded end to end for the project graph: the API returns every entity and the view relayouts the whole graph on each hover, which measured at 200-300ms per rebuild for ~6k nodes. Form validation generally mirrors backend schemas (question text, analysis code_version/method_hash/dataset_ids, commit message), while the cadence select and the 200-item single-page lists in the question refactor form drift from what the server can store. Detail-page mutations do not go stale on the home dashboard because app-shell toggles loadProjectData per route, which re-runs the workspace loader on return.

Strengths:
- useGraphDraftWorkflow enforces route identity (loadGenerationRef + isCurrent) and per-command in-flight refs so a stale draft can never be mutated or double-submitted; tests cover both.
- Bulk accept/undo is reversible and honest: buffered payload/note edits are persisted before accept-all, undo targets only ids from the latest bulk action, and the server's acceptance_mode remains the authority.
- useSourceArtifactPreviews bounds concurrency (4 workers), revokes every object URL on cleanup, and uses the shared authenticated blob transport; useReviewDictation releases the microphone on unmount and when edit rights are lost mid-recording.
- Draft permissions are derived from the draft's own project membership (and onboarding capabilities) rather than the dashboard selection, with the server as backstop.
- useProjectAccess, useProjectWorkspaceData, useProjectNoteData/SessionData and useAnalysisWorkflow all sequence requests with request ids so late responses cannot overwrite newer state.
- Network boundary validation (parseResource/parseCollection, gateway shapes) fails loudly on drifted envelopes, and fetchAllPages is used for unbounded lists in the workspace loaders.
- Frontend form validation matches backend schemas for questions, analyses (min 1 dataset, non-blank code_version/method_hash), sessions (primary question for scientific) and commit messages.

### Graph drafting pipeline (`graph-drafting`)

The graph-drafting area is a well-layered pipeline: provider clients (graph_drafting.py) produce a strict JSON graph patch behind a `GraphDraftClient` protocol; `GraphDraftGenerationCoordinator` claims a durable, lease-fenced generation row (CAS on claim token, renewals before every attempt, terminal writes fenced) before any provider I/O, validates the patch with `GraphPatchValidator` against the real API pydantic schemas plus entity-existence checks, and retries with trusted server feedback kept outside the untrusted context tags; `GraphDraftReviewCoordinator` owns the human gates (assignee/author keying, interactive-only accept, reserved `auto_accepted` rejected at service and repository layers); `TransactionalDraftCommitCoordinator` claims the row into COMMITTING and applies operations through the ordinary entity services inside one application transaction (rollback verified by tests); batch scheduling is split into pure policy (`batch_policy`), an atomic reviewer-note reservation with unique stable batch keys and Postgres advisory locks, and a claim/lease worker model mirrored for runs and change sets. Prompt-injection handling is careful (all note content JSON-encoded inside `<untrusted_*>` tags, system instructions to treat it as data, reviewer feedback and server feedback clearly labelled), provider errors are redacted at construction with configured secrets and generic key/header/query regexes and exception chains are cut, and provenance (provider/model/prompt version, origin ai_suggested vs user_revised, acceptance mode) is stamped consistently. Scheduling is correct across DST (verified next_run_at with America/New_York transitions) and dedupes via CAS on `next_run_at` plus stable batch keys. The fragile parts are at the seams: the commit coordinator carries onboarding-specific target rules that leak into general drafts (a validator-approved, accepted draft can be uncommittable), the generation-key idempotency has no escape hatch for rejected drafts, batch drafts cannot be revised although the UI offers it, and list/reservation paths still do whole-table reads and in-memory pagination that will not scale on hosted or long-lived instances. Authorization on the draft/batch routes is otherwise sound: contributor to draft/run, assignee/author (or owner …

Strengths:
- Generation ownership is durable and fenced: claim/renew/complete/fail are conditional updates keyed on a claim token and lease, checkpoints are committed before provider I/O even inside request transactions, and stale results can never overwrite a newer owner (covered by SQLite and Postgres fencing …
- Provider output is validated against the same pydantic request schemas the API uses, entity references are existence-checked, `$ref` placeholders are handled during review validation, and validation failures feed a bounded, clearly-labelled trusted retry instruction rather than being silently …
- Prompt-injection posture is explicit and consistent across providers: untrusted note/graph content is JSON-encoded inside delimiter tags, instructions tell the model to record instruction-like text as content, and revise prompts mark prior operations as untrusted while reviewer feedback is the only …
- Credential redaction is applied at the exception boundary (`GraphDraftingError` redacts on construction, transport exceptions are not chained), and persisted error metadata for runs, change sets and scheduler failures is passed through `provider_error_message` with configured secrets.
- The human gate is structural: `require_interactive` at accept/bulk-accept/commit, `auto_accepted` rejected at service and repository layers, owner-only commit, and review keyed on assignee with an explicit owner-recovery path for legacy unassigned batches; all covered by tests including SYSTEM and …
- Commit is atomic: claim to COMMITTING, deterministic lock ordering (deletion guard, question DAG, dataset/experiment locks), application through ordinary services with origin provenance, and a test proving canonical writes roll back on a mid-commit failure.
- Batch reservation is race-safe by design: reviewer-scoped advisory locks, disjoint note ownership across active runs, deterministic failed-retry key chains, legacy-key replay support, and unique-key backstops; concurrency tests exercise inline duplicate run-now calls and background rejoin.
- Scheduling handles timezones correctly (local wall-clock anchoring across DST verified), advances cursors only on successful runs, dedupes at the created_at boundary, and claims due settings with compare-and-set so external cron and the in-process ticker cannot double-fire.

### MCP server and tools (`mcp`)

The MCP area is a clean, standalone process layered on top of the HTTP API: `mcp_server.py` builds a FastMCP instance, `mcp_tools/read.py`/`write.py` are thin, uniformly-shaped adapters (each tool = one `LabTrackerAPIClient` call plus a structured `next_action` hint and a fail-soft error envelope), `mcp_api_client.py` owns wire mechanics via the shared `lab_tracker_client.transport.HttpTransport`, and `mcp_evidence_bundle.py` composes the atomic bundle endpoint with a snapshot-then-upload client-side follow-up. Authorization is deliberately kept at the API (LPAT policy in `auth.service_principal_can_access`, project scoping in services), which fits the layered design and keeps the MCP surface free of duplicated authz logic. The hosted path added in #202 is well built at the transport edge: fail-closed bearer middleware with constant-time comparison, single-header requirement, header stripping before dispatch, token-strength and distinct-token checks, loopback-only publishing and secret redaction in every error path. The fragility is in what sits between that edge and the API: identifiers are spliced into URL paths unescaped, so the tool surface is not actually bounded (any GET route, and other PATCH routes, are reachable); the hosted server still registers every write and local-file tool and trusts the operator to have minted a read-only LPAT; the startup open-ADMIN guard fails open on non-auth errors; and FastMCP's automatic DNS-rebinding Host check silently breaks the documented loopback-bind-behind-Caddy topology. Operationally, all tools are synchronous and execute inline on the event loop, which is fine for stdio but makes the multi-client hosted endpoint serialize on the slowest API call. Packaging is the most urgent issue: the dependabot range bump to `mcp<3` means any non-lockfile install (README, docs/setup.md, the documented `uvx --from git+...` launches) resolves mcp 2.2.0 and `lt-mcp` cannot import. Test coverage is broad and thoughtful for client semantics, redaction, artifact fail-closed behaviour and tool annotations, but it never exercises tool inputs containing path metacharacters, hosted mode with a non-loopback Host header, concurrent hosted calls, or the unavailable branch of the startup guard. Docs are mostly current (the generated tool …

Strengths:
- Inbound bearer gate (`MCPInboundBearerAuthMiddleware`) is fail-closed, uses `secrets.compare_digest`, rejects duplicate/malformed Authorization headers, strips the credential before FastMCP, and is covered by a real end-to-end streamable-http initialize/resource-read test.
- Startup refuses to run with a weak inbound token or one equal to the API LPAT (`_validated_inbound_token`, `_ensure_hosted_tokens_are_distinct`), and refuses a non-loopback auth-disabled API target.
- Secret hygiene: `redact_auth_secrets` scrubs `Bearer ...`/`lpat_...` from every error message and issue list; the runtime settings dataclass hides the token from repr; the Caddyfile redacts Authorization/Cookie from logs and sets no permissive CORS.
- Artifact resolution is fail-closed on both layers (`suppress_unverified_artifact_content` in client and tool) and content bounds are validated before any client acquisition, with schema-published limits and thorough parametrized tests.
- Read-only LPAT policy in `service_principal_can_access` is explicit and fail-closed (unknown scopes denied, `/auth*` denied, only two enumerated semantic-read POSTs), and a test proves a viewer LPAT reaches decision-context through the real MCP tool.
- Evidence-bundle client snapshots and fingerprints the local file before the atomic API call, verifies the uploaded asset against the fingerprint, and reports attachment failures explicitly without pretending to roll back.
- Tool annotations (readOnlyHint/destructiveHint/titles), `dry_run=True` default with mandatory idempotency key, and the 'AI can suggest; only a person commits' instructions are consistent across server instructions, quickstart resource, skill and docs.
- Actionable, self-describing auth errors (static-key rejection vs login rejection vs no credentials) with remediation, plus a stderr deprecation warning for username/password logins.

### Member onboarding and collaboration (`member-onboarding`)

The onboarding/collaboration area follows the repo's layered design cleanly: thin FastAPI routers (routes/member_onboarding.py, groups.py, projects.py, ownership.py, supervision.py) delegate through typed API mixins to services, and every project-scoped decision runs through one ProjectAuthorizationPolicy whose group-owner-inherits-owner / group_read_all-inherits-viewer semantics match docs/retained-v1-surface.md exactly (both membership_role and accessible_project_ids agree). Member onboarding is the strongest part: the checkpoint is a reserved-key immutable note, all mutation paths (put_checkpoint, put_manual_alignment, start_ai_alignment) are exact-replay idempotent, the manual path takes the project question DAG lock and finalizes via compare-and-swap on updated_at, AI alignment is fenced through generation claims, and validate_member_alignment_operations pins every proposal to the checkpoint project, so I found no cross-project leakage in context packets or accepted operations. Invitations (auth.py) are 256-bit random secrets stored only as SHA-256 hashes, admin-issued, email-bound, expiry-checked both in Python and in the conditional-UPDATE claim, and consumed atomically in the same transaction as user creation; the role is taken from the invitation, never from the registration payload, so role escalation through invitations is not possible; concurrency is covered by SQLite and Postgres tests. The fragile parts are the membership/group services: guards are implemented per-route rather than as invariants, so the same rule exists for one verb and not another (last-owner guard on group DELETE but not PATCH/POST; user-existence check on project PATCH but not group PATCH; owner lock on project membership but not group membership), and the offboarding "export before revoke" gate compares only project-id sets, not record freshness. Project-to-group attachment is one-directional in authorization: adding to a group requires the target group's owner, but detaching requires only a direct project owner, which lets a project owner unilaterally strip PI oversight that retained-v1 promises. Supervision edges and ownership reassignment are simple and admin/editor gated as designed, but supervision edges are instance-global and editable by any editor with no ownership …

Strengths:
- Member onboarding checkpoint is genuinely immutable and reserved: note_service refuses reserved metadata keys and the member-checkpoint: client_capture_id prefix from every generic create/patch/archive/delete path …
- validate_member_alignment_operations (src/lab_tracker/member_onboarding.py) enforces a closed contract: exactly one op per live question, only staged 'other' question creates in the checkpoint project or single additive links to active/staged same-project questions, all citing only the checkpoint …
- put_manual_alignment holds lock_project_question_dag and finalizes with try_finalize_member_onboarding_alignment(expected_updated_at) so concurrent alignment/first-capture/question-delete races are serialized; Postgres tests exercise the real lock ordering.
- Invitation tokens: secrets.token_urlsafe(32), stored hashed, admin-only issuance/listing/revocation, email-bound registration, role sourced from the invitation row, and single-use enforced by a conditional UPDATE (consumed_at IS NULL AND revoked_at IS NULL AND expires_at > now) inside the same …
- Project-membership last-owner guard uses lock_project_owner_memberships (SELECT ... FOR UPDATE) and re-reads after the lock, with a regression test that mutates under the lock (test_group_bulk_offboard_rechecks_owner_count_after_lock).
- Bulk group project-membership upsert rolls back partial saves on a sole-owner conflict (verified: project A membership not persisted when project B blocked).
- Device/service principal capabilities advertised by GET member-onboarding match middleware enforcement (capture-only devices, no commit for service tokens).

### Prior review regression check (H1-H12) (`prior-high`)

### Repository layer (`repository`)

The repository layer is a clean port/adapter split: `repository.py` defines `EntityRepository`, several focused protocols (notes, collections, data stores, graph change sets, review-email outbox) and the wide `LabTrackerRepository` facade, while `sqlalchemy_repository_parts/` implements them as per-domain classes composed by `SQLAlchemyLabTrackerRepository`. Query methods are consistently structured (separate count statement, `apply_pagination`, deterministic secondary ordering by id, escaped ILIKE substring search, early return for empty id sets), and child rows are hydrated in batches (`*_from_rows` + one IN query), so the obvious N+1 patterns are avoided (verified by the select-count test and by reading every `query`). Concurrency-sensitive paths (auto-transcription claims, onboarding checkpoints, generation/commit/batch-run/outbox claims) use conditional UPDATE ... WHERE with rowcount checks plus `FOR UPDATE`/`SKIP LOCKED` on Postgres and rely on SQLite's single writer otherwise; PostgreSQL advisory locks are domain-separated blake2b keys with documented canonical ordering, and the SQLite fallbacks (no-op, or a harmless project-row UPDATE as a write fence) are explicit and tested. Transaction boundaries are mostly caller-owned: repositories flush but never commit, except the explicit `commit/rollback/savepoint` facade, whose SQLite savepoint handling of the sqlite3 legacy transaction mode is careful and correct. The `GUID`/`EnumType`/`UtcDateTime` TypeDecorators make the mapper tolerant of str/UUID input, which I confirmed makes re-saving entities with loaded child rows work, but their strict bind-time `UUID()` parsing is the root of the two reproduced crashes: raw `created_by` filter strings reach GUID columns (HTTP 500) and non-UUID attribution strings break graph change-set hydration. The most consequential integrity gap is that ownership reassignment enumerates attribution tables by hand and misses eight of them, so the documented offboarding guarantee is not met and diverges from the export path. Other fragility is minor: swallowed root causes in data-store error classification, an unbounded/non-snapshot usage rollup, a silently ignored partial goal-target filter, and a few methods on concrete classes that are not in the protocol and are not …

Strengths:
- Batch hydration of child rows (`parent_map`, `target_map`, `dataset_map`, `_operations_for`, usernames) keeps list endpoints at a constant number of queries; covered by `test_note_repository_list_batches_child_queries`.
- Claim/lease mutations (`try_claim_auto_transcription`, `claim_for_generation`, `_finish_generation_claim`, `claim_next`, `_claim_row`, `finish_claim`, `claim_due`) are conditional UPDATEs fenced by tokens/lease expiry with rowcount checks and `expire_all()` after wins, so they are safe on both …
- Advisory-lock helpers hash the full UUID with a domain prefix, document canonical lock ordering (session -> experiment -> dataset/file), and are covered by unit tests on key stability plus Postgres integration tests for waits, rollbacks and deadlock-free ordering.
- Unique-constraint races are classified precisely (constraint names on Postgres, exact column lists on SQLite) and translated into typed port errors (`EvidenceBundleKeyRaceError`, `DataStoreNameRaceError`, ...) inside savepoints so the outer transaction stays usable.
- Append-only invariants (data stores, collection snapshots/manifests/captures, evidence bundles) are enforced in the repository itself, not just the service.
- Substring search escapes `%`, `_` and `\` and uses `ilike` with an explicit escape, closing LIKE-injection; UUID inputs are always stringified before binding.
- PATCH tri-state semantics (`NOT_PROVIDED` vs explicit null) are small, typed and end-to-end tested across questions, sessions, goals, claims, notes and exploration nodes.
- `repository_conventions.py` treats repo files as untrusted: symlink/traversal checks, git-tree (not worktree) reads, byte bounds, hash-verified metadata round-trip, all covered by tests.

### Review email and scheduling (`review-email-scheduling`)

The review-email subsystem is a clean transactional outbox: enqueue_ready_review runs inside the same application transaction that flips a GRAPH_BATCH change set to READY, rows carry a deterministic idempotency key with a unique constraint, and claim_next uses SELECT ... FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED on Postgres plus a conditional UPDATE so SQLite is also race-safe. The transport layer is deliberately contentless (fixed subject/body, only a signed link and an opaque digest), rejects CR/LF and credentialed URLs, sanitises every SMTP exception into a fixed error code, and hides the password from repr; the external bridge allowlists error codes and provider message ids so a mailbox automation cannot write arbitrary text into the DB. Capability gating (#181) is enforced at every layer (settings PATCH, enqueue, claim, test endpoint) and reviewer scoping (#189) is respected because deliveries are keyed on (change_set, review_assignee_user_id) and the address is re-read from that user's per-project row immediately before each attempt. The layering (routes -> API -> ReviewEmailService -> repository) is consistent and the scheduler's compare-and-set claim_due plus wall-clock ZoneInfo arithmetic in next_run_at handle DST correctly for daily cadences. The fragile spots are at the seams: address normalisation is not idempotent, so a rare-but-valid address poisons both the settings form and every batch run; the retry bound lives only in mark_failed, so lease-expiry reclaims are unbounded despite the docs; and the cron installer's stdout plumbing drops the trailing newline that Debian/Ubuntu crontab requires, so the documented Linux install path fails on real cron even though the stubbed test suite passes. Smaller hygiene issues include the claim_token being serialised in admin responses, a bare except in the signed-link route, unverified 'confirmed' addresses, and Windows credential handling that the docs gloss over.

Strengths:
- READY transition and outbox row are written in one transaction, with a unique idempotency key preventing duplicate enqueue (graph_draft_generation.py:618-623, migration 0057 uq_review_email_outbox_idempotency_key).
- Outbox leasing is atomic on both dialects: FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED on Postgres plus a conditional UPDATE guarded by the same eligibility predicate on SQLite.
- Email content is fixed and contentless; the transport dataclass rejects header injection, credentialed URLs and multi-address recipients, and tests assert no project/proposal words leak.
- All SMTP failures are mapped to a closed set of safe error codes with `from None`, so provider text (which may contain credentials) never reaches logs or the DB.
- External bridge sanitises provider_message_id (length, printable, single line) and only allowlisted error codes; the Compose control profile is least-privilege and tested for that.
- Host capability gating is enforced consistently: settings PATCH refuses opting in, enqueue/claim/test are all no-ops or errors when review_email_enabled is false.
- Preference and destination are re-validated immediately before each provider attempt so opt-outs cancel stale deliveries.
- Scheduler installer logic lives in a stdlib-only Python module with fail-closed crontab merging, strict charset validation for cron fields, JSON-serialised secrets in a 0600 file opened with O_NOFOLLOW, and adversarial-credential tests.
